LADIES' LETTER.

Mr Mau NELLIE. '4 The "Trelawny" hat—which fluttered through ) the season with a moderate popularity that seemed to augur but a short stay—is to remain for ' the autumn and winter with us, built in felts and - velvets. There is an amount of distinction about these shapes, but to be a real suecess they must be worn by a tall and stylish woman, as, like all large hats, they dwarf the fair of a few inches, and may also degenerate easily into dowdiness on a woman of homely appearance. A very fashionable hat is of dark grey felt, and is trimmed with twists and folds in front of grey velvet with white spots, from which spring two "'upright grey tips on the right side, and one long ,plume round the left, Fruit is still in high favour as decoration for our autumnal headgear, and a 'particularly pretty departure is evident at the 'present moment, it trails in bunches of black- berries. These combine delightfully with folds of white velvet, or on a purple (deep as the berries themselves) straw, with equally deep, purple velvet, mousseline-de-soie, or silk. The spotted Velvets are the fancy of the moment, black and 'coloured with white spots, or very light or white with black spots. Veils, it is said, may be worn shorter, but are not yet in evidence so.

A new toque has a crown of Parma violet miroir velvet, and a border of sable. On the left side are high bows of velvet, with a natural Paradise plume, and just behind this, resting on the hair, are some damask velvet roses in two shades of this very fashionable tone of red. The fur brim makes this model extremely becoming, and it is emi- nently suitable for wearing with the sable capes which will be among the most popular garments of the season. Now that a chill wave has succeeded the in- tense heat, and abruptly terminated a most bril- liant and "record" holiday season, interest will be taken in the autumn fashions. There is a decided inclination for the adoption of fur in the form of trimmings for gowns, coats, and mantles. High collars, revers, and cuffs of fur will figure on the now coats, while timinces of fur will be seen on the capes. Large fancy buttons are being in- troduced on coats and capes, whether they show a trimming of fur or not. Fur will be uzed in con- junction with lace for the most delicate of evening -gowns, and in millinery it will be seen mixed with tulle and chiffon.

Avery youthful and stylish little outdoor coat has large pearl buttons, revers of pearl-white moire, and the coat itself of light fawn-co' ared cl .th, stiohed with brown. The back fits quite cloeely, and has only one centre seam, but the front hangs straight and loose, and is kept fiat by an inter- lining of canvas. One of the prettiest, simplest, and most lady- like of autumn costumes is a black and white check, trimmed with black velvet ribbon. The well- fitting skirt has three rows of narrow velvet at the edge. The bodice, eut in a decollete square, has four large fancy buttons down the left side, where it fastens over, and three rows of velvet round the 'low square and at the edge of sleeves. A.. velvet waistband and a tucked chemisette of white silk gives the finishing touches to this dainty costume.

A very fashionable gown is of fine serge, braided with narrow black and white tubular braid. The west and revers are of silk, also edged with braid. This winter will bring us nearer to masculine costume in the growing narrowness of the cloth akirt, absolutely clinging to the hips like riding and cycling skirts, and made completely on the cross, leaving the width at the hem. This is not quite a divided shirt, but, very like it, both in facility of movement and lightness of material. iThe skirt should naturally be round to make it quite practical. It is never pleasant to be forced to raise one's skirt in the street, and cloth is ;specially adapted to street costume. One single skirt is worn. The coat bodice may be embroidered and:ornamented, but there should be no tunic, though we see it in some of the dressmaking estab- lishments. .

Your loving JNNEIB.

" WOMEN'S CHAT."

The Archduchess Gisela of Austria upon whom it is said the Pope intends to confer the Golden Rose, is the elder daughter of tbe late Empress Elizabeth. The Archduchess is one of the most charitable of women,and her unostentatious piety and domestic virtues endear her to all having the privilege of her acquaintance. The Pope's choice is a happy one,and is not likely to awaken envy, as is so often the case, among less favoured individuals.

The "Golden 11,-se " is, as its name suggests, a rose of gold. This is laid on the altar on Mid-Lent Sunday, and after haying been blessed by the Pope, annointed with rich perfumes. It is afterwards sent as a gift from the Pope to some Royal personage, church, or com- munity, which has been specially distinguished by acts of zeal for the church. If no one in any year is deemed worthy of the gift, it is laid up in the Vatican. Women preachers are supposed by many, to flourish solely in America. As a matter of fact, in many parts of this country "local preachers" of various demomina- tions, are women, and for directness of speaking, and sincerity of purpose it must be owned that they put many of our country clergymen to the blush. Carried away by their zeal, yet keeping well to the point, they convince their hearers as much by their air of sincerity and thoroughness, as by the doctrine they preach. For the eigth time, Lady Henry Somerset has been elected president of the British Women's Temperance Association. That this association has become so power- ful and influential a body, is undoubtedly owing to it 3 Presidents efforts. " Simpliuity and thoroughness " is her working motto, and she never allows anything con- nected with the temperance movement to be scamped or ill-done. It would be a mistake however, to imagine , that Lady Henry Somerset gives up all her time to tem- perance work. All that concerns and benefits her own sex is of the keenest mement to her, and she has always been one of the best and most thoughtful speakers at the yearly meetings of the Central Conference of Women Workers.

Lady Henry Somerset makes no boast of her ancestry but she is no; the less justly proud of her descent from two of the most devoect friends of Marie Antoinette— the Chevalier and Madame d'Etang. They fled from Paris, during the Reign of Terror, and founded, in Eng- land, the family of which Lady Henry Somerset is pro- bably the best known representative. Notwithstanding the many claims made on her by her public life, and work, Lady Henry finds time to be a charming hostess to her many friends and acquaintances. She is still a young-looking woman, as many of those who have seen or heard her speaking in public meetings for the cause she has so much at heart, can testify, and appears more like the sister than the mother of her twenty-four year old son. A young man of great premise, who will probably ultimately succeed to the Dukedom of Bean- fort. _ _

To be a good speaker, one must speak from a full mind, and know thoroughly the subject one is discus- sing. And not only that subject, but the collateral topics which would enliven it, eft illustrate it, or make it clearer. Then comes the absolute necessity of luci- dity. An individual even of less information, with the ability to state his or her own views, so that the crdinary understandMe immediately comprehends the thought, will often distance—in debate—a very much better equipped antagonist. The Marchioness of Tweeidale, who has lately beon playing the role of hostess to the Duke and Duchess of Connaught, at Y.ster House, Haddiegtonshire, is a woman of bright intelligence, and wide sympathies, and is fully in touch with all sorts of social questions in which royal personages may be interested. tlcr mother was Scotch, her father a member of a distinguished Roman family. She posiesses the fine eyes and hair of the South, dresses exquisitely, and is a Yemen of intake tact. Her only daughter, Lady Clementine Hay, who was presented last season, is an exceedingly pretty vivacieus girl, and a great favourite wherever she goes. Yester House is charmingly situated, and surrounded by beautiful gardens. In the grounds lie the famous cave, where the encounter took place between Marmion and his elfin foe, in Sir Walter Scott's poem of that name. The ancient Castle of Yester stood near the Lammermuir Hills, and the remains of it are still to b 3 seen on a peninsula formed by two streams. The attempt to introduce the short veil, dees not promise to be a SUCCess. We all know the comfort of a veil that covers the face in winter, and wears not likely to patronise the " newest idea" at this season of the year. The latest veilings are arranged with irregular chenille spots. On black net the spots are white, and on white net they are black. It is necessary to exercise some care in putting these veilings on. The result of a white spot reposing somewhere about the tip of one's nose is, that the unfortunate feature appears rosy by contrast, while a black spot near a tooth, gives the undesirable aspect of one missing. Some women drese cleverly just as other women talk, Cr write, shop, or keep house cleverly. There is always a way of doing everything cleverly, and the women who dress cleverly always wear gowns suitable fo.t the season, aud the occasion. There is a certain dash of novelty, and even originality in their gowns, and their toilettes have a certain air of freshness about them. A clever dresser will always lay in a good stock of vests and fronts to go with her tailor-made coats and Shirts, end her more dressy holeros,knowing full well the value of these meinl little articles. How many changes it is possible to ring if possessed of a few dainty vests, and the little iteras—such as inside skirt frills, wrist frills, waiethelts, etc.,—neeessary to carry out each soheme of color! A middle-agerd gown can easily be fresneael up, and made quite un ecognisabie, by such simple means as these. One of the vests destined to be worn with a skirt and coat of black cloth, by a young,graceful Duchess. which I saw a few days back is in blade satin. The fronts are left open to show an undervest of white satin,and this is also left open to allow the jabot frills of the large wbite crepe de chine cravat to spring out. Yet another vest is of neliotrope mousseline de soie, with rather large white satin spots on it. It is made up over heliotrope satin, and this same fabric is wound round,the throat,and tied in a narrow bow in front. From the middle of the bow spring frille of white satin, edged with chiffon, which reach almost te the waist. As a means of illumination, candles have been advo cated strongly in many quarters of late, and deservedly. for the light of a candle is, in itself, the most agreeable known. The candle, however, requires a good deal of care. If burned through the whole evening it must be renewed every day. It is possible though, to eet very

large wax candles, such as are used in churches, which will burn for a great many evenings, and require no at tention. For the dinner table nothing better than can dles,carefully shaded,has been found. A lamp is clumsy, and obstructs the view, while overhead lighting usually gets in the eyes. Furthermore,the candles,in their silver sticks,with their small coloured shades,are in themselves a charming decoration. Artificial flowers are being extensively patronised in America for table, ball-room, and house decoration. The blooms—of muslin—are exquisitely finished, and only the closest inspection reveals the fact that they are not , Nature's own handiwork. It is not very probable though that hostesses in this country will take them up. We suffered too much from the terrible wool and wax flower period to recover easily and face the prospect of such monstrosities again invading our domestic peace. In these days, when pretty serving is such a consider- ation, it may be as well to warn housekeepers, that when one has to reheat food in a silver dish,from which it is impossible to move the eatable, considerable care is necessary,if we would preserve our property. The best plan is to half fill a baking tin with hot water, place in this a doubled sheet of paper, and stand the silver dish upon it. It will then take no harm from the effects of the oven heat. Scrambled Haddock is an excel'ent breakfast dish. Boil a dried haddock in water, for a few moments until it comes away from the bone easily. Drain, scrape all the fish fr e from the skin ond bone,and place in a small saucepan with a little butter,milk,and a dash of pepper. Stir it round until very hot, spread on rounds of toast, and serve immediately. " MADGE ".

LIVERPOOL CONRSTORY COURT.

A meeting of the Consistory Court was held on Tues- day afternoon, at St. Nicholas's Parish Church, Chancel liar Espin presiding. WARRINGTON P OISE{ CEIURCEL An application was made on behalf of the widow af the late John Richard Picknoere for authority to erect a memorial tablet to her husband in the south wall of the church.—The faculty wag decreed. ST. FEHR'S NEWTON-IN-MAKERFIELD. This was an application by the vicar and church- wardens of St. Peterra, Nevaton-in-Makerfield by authority to place four stained-glass windows in the church, two In memory of George M'Corquodale, one In memory of Harriet B. Watkins, and the remainder In memory of Geerge Armitage.—The faculty was granted.

At Northampton on Tuesday Samuel Tomlinson, alivs Fred Teralinson. alias Harry Smith, was committed for trial on a charge of attempting to wreck the Birmingham expreas on the London and North-Western Ittilway at fluntsbury Hill. Other charges of alleged tilt' n-wrecking Were adjourned.

On Tuesday afternoon. shortly after three o'clock, Mr. John Holmes, aged sixty-seven, a retired joiner, living at 66. Prenchwood-street. Preston, was struck by lightbing and instantaneously killed during a thunderstorm of only ten minutes' duration, Mr. Holmes took shelter in the archway of Messrs. Allsopp's ironworks, and while standing there met his death.

Sir Timothy H. O'Brien, the well-known Middleiex cricketer, has met with a serious accident while hunting with the Dohallow hounds in Ireland. He was thrown heavily into a blind ditch, and was picked up insensible. The muscles of hi, shoulder were lacerated. He was removed to the residence of a neighbouring gentlemen, where be Is pro- greasing favourably.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, OCTOBER 14 1898

"Ye're too susceptioie to the designs o' this fascinating wuman," I muttered angrily, turning on ray heels and catching Lucia's eyes as they wandered pleadingly from Mark to me. She was sharp enough to c-mprehend what was passing, from our manu,” n the few words she understood, so she a. Mark with timidity, saying: "If I have the courage, who shall deny my right? If I face the hardships unflinchingly, and walk and ride abreast your companions, surely you are chivalrous enough to welcome my songs, music, and dancing? Here, in Yerba Buena, I am courted by all classes for my accomplishments. Surely they will be as acceptable as your friend's kilts and bagpipes ? " This gentle sarcasm fairly bowled me over, for I seldom played, and my kilts were stowed away sa4 interlined with tobacco leaves to keep the „.‘,4olis out. it that moment the gallop of an approaching smstang arrested our attention, and on looking out of the window I saw the best horseman on Mark's ranch at San Leno tearing towards the saloon at full speed. "By the condition o' the l'animal," observed T, "an' the roughness o' the rider, it's evident he's ridden lang an' continuously." The Californians were shrewd enough seldom to jeopardise themselves in the midst of danger by sending written despatches, so when the messenger threw himself from his mustang and came striding into Puke s Delight I opened our door quickly to let him in. His tidings were delivered with that pleasing intonation which the Californian language permitted, for even the commonest bullock-driver delivered a message like a skilled ambassador at an imperial audience. The substance of the mes- sage was that Indians were in force, threatening San Leno, and assistance was necessary for the defence of the station.

This brought the question of Lucia's request to a climax. Having heard the news, she mustered up all her courage and declared that she would either join us or follow us whithersoever we went. When grunting out my consent, and wishing the Senorita joy of her choice, I left as awkward as when I first put the breeches on, for the grateful Spaniard threw her arms about my neck, and before I could find the presence of mind to resist she had kissed my scrubby cheek. I blushed as I Orked my body upright to avoid a second attempt, and Mark roared with laughter at what he termed "The celibate's discomfiture ; a humorous incident, fit for the brush of a Royal Academician." No man with any gallantry could resist the will of this woman, who, in her affection, was prepared to brave storm and drought, the white highway- man's surprises and the Indian's treachery. Her soul seei.led moved with a love for my friend which I am conscious my words inadequately describe. It surpassed my comprehension, and was beyond my modest ability to fathom. As for Mark's devo- tion, it seemed a sin to doubt its constancy. Lucia had conquered. We were to press forward at once for the relief of San Leno.

Acting upon the business principle that it is best to give good wages and so obtain first-class skill, I engaged only the smartest men, so that as our preparations for a speedy movement across the passes of the coast got noised abroaxi, the more venturesomc from the whole countryside volunteered their services. Youths of spirit and dash implored me to enrol them. Those passionately fond of horse-racing, and who had impoverished them- selves by making extravagant wagers, were like- wise importunate for a place on the muster. I refused applications from notorious gamesters, for they drove a thriving business and netted the pro- ducts of rancheros. Mere adventurers we tabooed, but as the Senorita "arsisted in accompanying us, the wives of several settlers joined their husbands, and I took care to gather a band large enough to afford mutual protection against hostile Indians. Our scouts would have their work cut out to glean tidings of the Indians concerned in the mysterious disappearance of Pecos; and such was the nature of the so-called roads that, without extraordinary delays or detentions, San Leno could not be reached by our advance party in lees than a week. Contingencies, such as cattle giving out or waggons breaking down, might require the utmost fraternity and good-will, besides handicraft of a useful nature, so I selected a few intelligent artisans. Of course, we were not short of dollars, and from our stores we drew tobacco, trinkets, and beads for presents to friendly Indians. Mark's projected scheme of capturing Queen Hans compelled the enrolment of crack shots, and the more dashing of them were selected to precede the main body for the immediate relief of San Leno. The wisdom of this step was afterwards proved. Now Mark was to lead this advance guard, with me as his first lieutenant, and if in reconnoitricg and F-onring the country we discovered likely dar r, we were to draw in settlers from outlying dis jot, and so augment this scouting squadron. All ie precautions were absolutely necessary, for the red man was hardly tolerant to the palefa^e, and in an uncivilised country mere brute force was often victorious where argiurans and kind less tied. We pioneers of days which are now but as a memory were a motley company. We discarded all articles likely to encumber our journey. Our powder was dry, our weapons sharpened, cleaned and primed; our kit adequate, and our spirits high and determined. Each man signed a contract enforcing strict obedience; but, to my mind, the most stimulating part of the whole business was the fact that we were engaged in a righteous and just cause. In view of coming events it is necessary to note that the smartest Indian in Yerba Buena acted as guide to our scouts, for the trails were scarcely traceable by white men, and the unshod Indian ponies left no appreciable sign for our guidance. We got away as soon as pcssible, and our dusky pilot's knowledge of the topography _of the coast range was unique and valuable. He knew the slight abrasions of the hard rocks, displaced stones, loosened mounds of earth, bent weeds and burnt bushes by which the red men followed the short cuts to safe retreats. Now it chanced on a lovely May morning, after Mark had insisted on Lucia Diaz following on with the main body, that we of the advance guard followed the Indian's lead as he wound among the foot-hills, densely clothed with chaparral and flowering shrubs. His keen eye noted certain large clumps of horse-chestnut and ilex ; and while we were admiring the rainbow hues obich tinged the grassy slopes where the banks of flowers were opening to the sunlight, the native was taking the bearings of forest aisles, and watching for strips of yellow-grey lichen hanging in festoons from the trunks of large trees. Ile was counting pale wreaths termed "Old Man's Beard," or marking certain trees which the wood- peckers had honeycombed with bullet-like holes to store their winter food. Whether we were passing in the cool green shade of some miniature ravine, or picking our way through a small virgin forest, we felt we were as safe from harm as robin red- breasts at home.

Nothing occurred to hinder us during the first few hours, save the ugly fall of a mustang and a nasty abrasion to the arm of the rider. Our guide hunted for foliage something like beech, and plucking several leaves tied them over the wound. It was probably a species of the Balm of Gilead tree, which the Indians about the Sierra Madre value for tha healing resin its leaf-buds exude. The air was warm and dry, game plentiful, and rabbit-shooting our diversion. The rabbits were boiled in a saucepan which the pack-horses carried, and it is almost superfluous to state that our appetites were generally keen. A cross-country route brought us by noon to a lovely wooded spot where the spray of a small waterfall dazzled in tints of diamonds, emeralds, opals and sapphires. The froth sparkled like dancing stars as the foam- water splashed and uhirled and raged down ,o 0 rocks and did battle with the black boulders cauldron below. Then above the tiny whirl- fleecy clouds of vapour sparkled in the sun, and the hanging mist cooled the air where we camped and took our meal. After a refreshing rest our guide mounted the rocks to view the country. While standing there he fancied he heard a distant signal, and when he rejoined us and urged us to press on we quickly obeyed the sum- mons, for the red man's hearing was as wonderful as his cunning. Faithful Jip was well in tow till we reached a lateral gorge, when a noise between a bloodhound's bay and an African lion's roar made him prick his ears. The whole of us proceeded with extreme caution. Preserltly, at a sign from the Indian guide, we halted; and he drove his unshod pony a hundred yards ahead, dismounted quickly, threw himself flat on the earth, and put his ear to the ground. In less than three minutes he sprang to his saddle, and, wheeling to the right, was out of sight among the trees and crags. The next moment he uttered syo rbeostt, lneds es at h aotn,,M,aar rdk, alaentwd himaar nti thenfgre es: elalnne triijleiin)ll3te dai wasan at immediately lioness passed

Mark took aim and fired. The lioness gave a tremendous leap, Jip closing with her. I never law a fiercer struggle, but it lasted only 11 few moments, for soon the life of the mountain-rover ebbed, and :Tip had an easy task. None of us dared fire a second shot for fear of striking the courageous bloodhound, but we rejoiced to see Jip escape with very little damage. With assist ance our guide dragged the dead lioness to a dry gully, and, pushing the body therein, covered it with brushwood and leaves. Jip bounded on the scent, and soon we discovered the carcase cf a sheep, weighing nearly three hundred pounds, which the lioness had dragged for miles from the valley to its den in the rocks. We left both animals for the wild coyotes and wolves which still infested the range, and then pressed on with energy through the pass towards the threatened garrison.

CHAPTER VI. A STRANGE ADVERTISEWENT.

A surprise was now in store for us, for about, mile to the north on the rising ground, and e' -- angle of about forty-five degrees with our coot— we saw two persons on horseback. Mark lookeo through his glass, and observed: "Father Otando on a mule, and the Senorita Lucia Diaz on her Pinto. They are on the crow's-fly trail betweeti Yerba Buena and San Leno." My exclamation was more forcible than polite, but as they were vigorously Waving their handker- chiefs we turned in their direction, trusting to Jip's sagacity and the Indian guide for warning of danger. On reaching them, Lucia made no bones about telling us there was a crisis at the San Leno ranch, and that we lingered for enjoyment because new pastures fatten calves. This was her style of reproof. She conferred aside with my chief. As I beheld her loveliness I wondered not that Mark Pepys was dazzled and blinded by her surpassing charms. Nature had gifted her with resistless attractions—men far and wide extolled her grace, the delicate cream of her face, with the faintest tinge and gloss of a light scarlet geranium upon her cheeks ; her dark lustrous eyes radiant and beautiful as a fawn's, her raven tresses as black and shiny as eagles' feathers, her swan-like neck and bosom as white as marble, the flesh of her small hands and tapering fingers as soft as that Of a babe. The secret sorrow upon Mark's soul was chased away by the sunshine of her merriment. This bright star of the Far West, whose crystal light paled not as the moon rose, set Mark PePYs'. strong nerves vibrating, and gave a glow of prid, and pleasure to his manly features. His full dart eyes now feasted upon a beauty "Whose cheek had the pale pearly pink Of sea-shells—the world's sweetest tint ; As though she lived, one half might deem.

On rose sopped in pearly dew." Mark presently turned from Lueia and said . "Pecos is suffering torture in a small Indian camp away south-east. It is our duty to rescue him. I am willing to face the danger; are you ready? " "Aye, ayel" resounded on all sides; and for- ward we rode, urged by a zeal which no ordinary obstacle could thwart. Two hours after sunset we reached a solidly-built adobe dwelling thatched with thule, one storey high, with a slightly slanting roof. It stood in the midst of a well-kept garden, rich and fragrant. A gruff inquiry came from the Quaker tenant, asking our business. He peered out of the window, satisfied himself that our intentions were honour- able, and came down. "Here, lassie!" said he on opening the door, "see thee to the comfort of the Senorita." This latter to his wife, a buxom, red- face dame. After a hasty meal fresh ponies were saddled, and Mark observed to Lucia: "Senorita, you are tired, and need rest. Stay here till we return." "Thou art welcome, lass," added the Quaker. "By the way, sir, a traveller from the old countr, was this way making diligent inquiries about thee. Thou art surprised, friend! He left me two or three copies of the Times. But more about that on thy return." A sudden inspiration advised me to seize those papers, but Mark forestalled me with: "Hand me the newspapers. Mayhap we shall need to halt, and they will while away the time." A deaf person often comprehends more than we suspect, and Lucia made tip for her imperfect knowledge of English by comprehending every look and sign. These, with the words she understood, enabled her to obtain an inkling which I could see had piqued her curiosity. The singular use she afterwards made of this confirmed my surmise. All nature aided our mission, for the moon was full and bright, the air breezy, and the scent good. I shall never forget the heartiness with which our lads entered into the spirit of this search, and the joy depicted on every face as our Indian leader and Jip indicated the whereabouts of the enemy. We dismounted, tethered our horses, and with the

• utmost caution crept forward. Presently we saw some smouldering embers among a clump of silver pines in a rugged hollow, and, after priming our weapons, we stole a march on the unwary. "Mark," I whispered, "there is Pecos, half clad, his wrists tied behind him, an' lashed to a tree." Ile was apparently sleeping from sheer exhaus- tion, for his chin rested on his breast, and his body had dropped till it was all of a heap. I. have known Indians take turns to keep a captive awake, a species of cruelty which produces inde- scribable exhaustion and misery. Inch by inch Mark crawled to release Pecos, taking care that when cut down the hunchback should be borne to the most shaded part of the thicket, for the glare of the fire would make us better targets for Indian arrows. Well, we were within twenty feet of the captive when the "whoop!" of a startled redskin rang through the woods. The fellow was picked off by one of our crack shots, and Mark and I made a rush for Pecos, whose cords we severed in a trice. He seemed paralysed with fear; but when he saw the confusion in the Indian camp, and heard the shots from our rifles and the howls and thuds of several merciless wretches whom we hit, he guessed the truth, and with a deep sigh fainted in Mark's arms. The surprise was perfect, not a single scratch being received by our party; while a half score of the Indians who shewed fight had bitten the dust. No effort was made to slaughter those who fled, nor did we search among the brushwood, for fear of the stragglers' revenge. Pecos soon revived, for he suffered nothing beyond emaciation from exposure, semi-starvation, and rough usage. "Pecos," I asked, "are any of the White Chief's treasures in the camp? " "I know nought of them," answered the dazed man. Mark's face dropped with disappointment, bir he instantly gave the order: "Cut the Indians ponies loose, and drive them before us. W. must effect our escape before the redskins retur— to ascertain the mischief and our strength." This accomplished, we arrived without mishap at the settler's house. Pecos was given bear fat oil, which soon freed him from pain, and restored his appetite and normal vitality. His story was a singular one. "These Indians," said he, "treacherously attacked Puke's Delight, and I fled. I met the labourer in the field near the Presidio, and he gave me shelter in his shanty; but two red men discovered me, and tried to kid- nap me. Thereupon the labourer stood by me, shouting and holding on to my beads and singlet until he was stabbed. Then I was gagged, blind. folded, and carried whither I knew not. My captors spat at me, slapped my face, and made mockery of my deformity. Then they half starved me, and beat me to tell all I knew about Sal Leno ; but I was silent. Then they debated how I should die, and what means they should adopt, k) remove all traces of my body. But they quarrelled, and I was left for a while. It was then that the White Chief and his party came and rescued me. Pecos will shew his gratitude by acts rather than words." Concerning the whereabouts of Mark's curios Pecos was entirely ignorant, but from casual remarks overheard he judged the redskins were in longue with those whose threatening attituel.

• - alarmed the people at San Leno. As we intern,. gated, Lucia watched us keenly. "Harry Puke was gagged," observed Mark, lool— ing with intense earnestness at the hunchback .•• note. the expression of his face; "and we four._ him strapped down in his own cellar." Pecos indignantly retorted: "Thinkest thou Pecos's:brain is waste ground to receive everybody's rubbish!" "Well," urged Mark, "his stock was stolen as well as my treasures Can you throw any light on this? "

Pecos shook his head with dumb surprise, and after thinking awhile he broke the silence with : "I saw none drunk, nor a cigar or bottle in their camp." He was emphatic on the point, and we had no reason to disbelieve him. "Did'at thou see the Castilian, named Muro ? " I asked. We looked dubious, La ne stuck to the negative. SECRET OF MARK PEPYS.

BY FRED J. PROCTOR, Author of "Timothy Twill's Secret," "Jean

liarlou," '`"Dr. Dobbs, " Scc.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: This story is related by Sandy Dees, a Californian trader before the days of the gold rush. His employer, Mark Pepys, is in love with a Mexican girl, Lucia Diaz. Manuel Muro, a Mexican, also appears to love the girl, and Dees fears that he is plotting to take Pepys's life. He doubts the girl's good faith also. One night Pepys's store is ransacked and a half- breed on guard is killed. There is reason to believe that Muro and a band of Indians have had a hand in the affair. Pepys takes an oath to revenge the half-breed. Sandy Dees discovers that Pepys has a secret which prevents him ever returning to his native land, and the Scot tries to discover its nature.

===== 0002601_18981014_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
CIIAPTER V.

HOW LUCIA CONQUERED. Now Lucia Diaz wished to join our expedition, and, being a very impressionable woman, the task of curbing her high-bred nature was a difficult one. Mark shrugged his shoulders and pulled a long face, looking at me imploringly; but I shook my head.

"0 Mark!" she cried, brushing the tears from her iluslud face, which was the picture of winsome misery, "you have a poor estimate of a woman's love. If you make San Leno your headquarters in this raid—"

"It is no raid, Senorita," said I emphatically. "Then it is revenge—the Californian vendetta,' she retorted; "a revenge which robs me of my lover, and throws a cloud over the happiness of my life. 0 Mark! Mark!" she implored, "separa- tion would mean death for me."

"Nonsense, Lucia, you have too much courage for that," said Mark warmly. "Suicide is a coward's release."

Advancing with dignity, her breast heaving, and the tears flowing down her cheeks in spite of her resolution to conquer them, she cried "Why have you won my heart, to throw it aside as I would a soiled fan after a ball? Is my love naught to you, that you should trample my heart under your feet? Then, fiercely drawing her supple figure to its full height: "Who, think you, would care for Lucia Diaz, after the elite of California have coupled our names, and the Press, even in Wash- ington and New York, has spoken in glowing terms of our betrothal. And your excellent friend," she added, wheeling round with flashing eyes, "is the barrier between us—the cold and hard frost chilling your love! Why do you listen to this man, who would take you from me, break my heart, and per- haps gull you to your destruction? He shall not triumph! " she added, a vague anxiety shadowing her lovely face, as she sprang forward like an angry leopardess. Then, seeing the smile which unconsciously flitted across my face, she added : "I would kill you first! " "So ho, Mark!" I observed in English, as I shook myself free of her vice-like grip; "I said ye would hae trouble wi' this fiery beauty. You'll be the spinning-top, she'll whip." With one stride Mark was by her side, and lay- ing his hand firmly on her shoulder, he said : "Lucia, Sandy is my friend. You are acting like a petulant child." "0 Mark! " she cried, falling down and clasp- ing his knees, "forgive me, dear; I did not mean to pain you!" Then springing to her feet, a sudden red flush upon her cheeks, she threw herself on the sofa, and burying her face in the pillow swayed to and fro as though in deep mental distress. "Well, I'm hanged if I know what to do," said Mark gloomily; "she doesn't understand the dangers." "El corazon manda las carnes "she cried, guessing Mark's meaning. "The heart bears up the body," I observed; "she's a veritable Joan o' Arc on the Pacific coast "—here my voice was laden with sarcasm— "to whom toil must be sweet, an' recklessness honey, while the man o' her choice is tied to her apron strings. Pardon me, Mark," I added, as his eyes kindled and the Senorita, quick to com- prehend, disdainfully looked at me. "The.pro- position o' a lady joining us is so outrageous that rim, words o' mine are sufficiently strong to condemn the idea. It's madness far the Senorita to go. If speedy flight becomes necessary, she will be a drag upon us, an' a clog upon our movements every- whaur. Wumen to their duties an' place, is my motto."

"The fact is," retorted Mark, with better grace than my temper warranted, "you are a hater of the sex, and if she were an heroic nurse—a Florence Nightingale—you would turn up your nose at her. Now, lam not at all disintlined to listen. Come now, Sandy, waive your objection for once." The stronger the man the greater the influence of a pretty woman. "An' she goes," I urged, as though predestination were shaping our lives, "meeschief will brew sooner or later. An' so ye maun juist sing ems' to her tantrums! " "Oh, you always shout for water before the fire breaks out," he replied, roughly knocking down the finger with which I pointed at him. "You are an old. croaker! It is difficult to tell where diplomacy ends and deceit begins." "I've nee patience wi' a sane man running into danger," said I, with a bitterness which made him wince; "but there's some excuse for a lunatic." "So I thought," observed Mark, with galling irony, "when you were cajoled into visiting an Indian camp,. and got dead drunk on their own brewing. Look here, Sandy, I hate to see a woman in tears. We must come to some decision."

"My mind's no moved," said I emphatically; "so tak' the mistak' on yersel'. True, she sing!: richt well, but she canna wash a mutch, I waurant ! Lucia evidently guessed the purport of our con- versation, for she sprang to her feet, and, seizing my arm, said pathetically: "Why do you stand between us? " Then suddenly raising her voice she passionately cried : "There is peace in friendship, but danger in meddling! Is there some fair lily in yonder valley of which Pecos spoke, and will her love be to Mark as the spring showers and warm sunshine to the lovely flowers of Golden Vale? Beware, Mark, how you trifle with a Castilian's love!"

And turning upon me with flashing eyes she hissed between her pearly teeth: " Quien 'much° abraza pow aprieta ! " With a grimace at me Mark muttered: "Who grasps much holds little. Cave in, Sandy; she wins by a neck." Now Lucia walked disdainfully from the room, beautiful even in her temper. "Ye begin to see the claws o' the graceful panther," I observed, somewhat gingerly. "Scratch a Castilian an' ye'll find the de'il." "I anticipated this tempest, for, like the sun at sunrise, she was pale this morning, and the expres- sion of her face undefined." Upon hearing this admission I again advised him to abandon the idea of taking her with us; but he sent me headlong to a much warmer place than a Yellowstone geyser. .Lucia's rippling laughter silenced us as she came tripping into the room, her eyes dry, her bright face bathed in smiles, and her ruffled curls adding glory to her beautiful figure. "Forgive me, darling," she said, embracing Mark, in spite of my menacing countenance and his embarrassment. "I was wild and wicked to say such unkind things. It was a wilful fit of temper to get my own way, and I am ashamed of myself." "Lucia," observed her stalwart lover, accepting her contrition and kissing her forehead, "you are like a spoilt child; and tho more I give way to you, the harder it is to refuse you anything. Sandy," he added in English, "the man who swears most against petticoat government is the first to yield to its influence. Bow at the shrine of beauty." As a direct descendant of a puritanical Scotch family, I felt very uncomfortable as she fondly drew his arm about her waist, laid her cheek on his broad chest, and slyly looked at me from the corners of her scintillating eyes, as much as to say: "Ah, see; I have my own way in spite of you." She then tossed her head and laughed, observing in Spanish idiom what I gathered to mean "Happy the woman who is queen of a good man's heart." "Well, Sandy," said Mark, his face puzzled, and his voice full of doubt, "to be or not to be ? " "We're wasting precious time," I replied, look- ing dolefully out of the window and purposely turning my back to the suspicious Lucia. "Settle it ony way ye like." "Seeing that San Leno will be the base of opera- tions," he argued, "how would it be to give her charge of the ambulance and hospital? A woman of ready resource is always useful." "She canna either set a broken bone or amputate a limb," I answered peevishly. "She'll mak' a line muckle." Mark smiled, and replied: "Ah, but she can hold the bandages and make poultices."

°No uncommon Inane, " answered recos, loosing somewhat bewildered by reason of our wrapt attention.

After probing the hunchback's memory in va;n Mark busied himself scanning those copies of the Times left by the Quaker's visitor, and I made bold to closely watch the effect of his perusal of several paragraphs. Lucia also evinced considerable interest in Mark's face. Presently he took out his knife, and cut out some advertisements, care- lessly tearing them up and dropping the pieces on the fireless hearth. He made a note in his pocket- book of the date of the newspaper, and evidently some reference to the special item. Not antici- pating watchers, he put the papers back on the side-table, and, after yawning, he endeavoured to snatch some sleep. Thereupon I casually took up the papers and found the eliminated advertise- ments had been marked round with blue pencil. They were among the legal notices, and as Mark had no blue pencil, I guessed the stranger from England had marked the papers. The mysterious dread which Mark apparently had of meeting! British detectives had excited my curiosity. "I must pick up the pieces," thought I, "for, if great trouble looms ahead for may boss,' I may be in a position to help or advise him. And yet there would not be anything very significant in the legal advertisements, or he had lit his pipe with them, and so destroyed the chance of a second party pasting the pieces together and reading the printed matter." I lingered over the papers a long while, and found my chin dropping on my chest several times. Then I passed near the hearth and stooped, but the bits of newspaper had disappeared! Lucia had gone to bed with the Quaker's wife, and no doubt she had foiled my purpose completely by slyly picking up the bits while I dozed. "Trust a wuman fer sagacity an' smartness. Like a fule I hae lost my chance. I wonder what method I can adopt to obtain possession o' those pieces? Can I onyhow worst the Spanish wuman ? " I had often worked out a proposition of Euclid in the dark as I lay in bed, but there was no chance of solving this question to-night, for, like the five wise virgins, we kept our lamps trimmed. At sunrise we revisited the scene of our night's adventure, and found the Indians had secured the dead and struck camp. This stimulated us to fresh exertion, and as Jip and our guide's experience convinced us the red men had gone by a detour in the direction of San Leno, we now took a straight course for that place, hoping to get within the ramparts before a-iy serious attack was con- templated.

I shall never forget the hurrahs, waving of handkerchiefs and hats, and the commotion among the garrison as we hove within range of their guns. "Thank God! we're juist i' the nick o' time," I observed to Mark, as we urged our fagged horses down the rough road. "The Indians are buzzing like bees round a honey-pot," he-answered. "If our missionaries would stop hame an' convert the heathen i' the slums o' our great cities," I argued, "it would be wiser than introducing muskets, powder, an' bullets amang Indian tribes fer our destruction." , "Missionaries are blamed for much,"Mars retorted, "that traders deserve. We have taken the besiegers by surprise, but if we get within the fort safe and sound, I think we can teach the rebels a lesson worth remembering. Lads, look to your firearms!" Just then an arrow sped from the door of an Indian hut, and struck the pommel of my saddle. "Crack! crack!" answered from the ramparts, , followed by the boom of a howitzer; and then the , whizz of arrows and crisp rifle-shots from the 1 red men.

A mustang was ;knocked over, the horseman rushing towards the fort on foot. Lucia DiaS miraculously escaped a hit from a bullet which slit her pony's ear; and Mark's wrist was grazed with an arrow.

A volley from the ramparts and a few shots from our party caused a stampede among the enemy. Covering our passage by a second volley, the garrison unbarred the gates, and time cannot efface the scene from my memory. Tears of joy trickled down the faces of even the sturdiest men as we rode safely within the walls. The grateful thanks of the women repaid us amply for the risk; and even the dear children danced and shouted them- selves hoarse, for they quickly comprehended that the deliverance of their parents was also their own, safety. As some of the men shook our hands their lips tried to shape words of congratulation, but great lumps rose in their throats, and the only thing they could do was to grasp our hands long and fervently. Some, whose age reminded me of the sear and yellow leaf of autumn, and who had stood adversity with heroic resignation, now sobbed aloud for joy. Mark, whose good-nature and courage had hitherto won admiration, was now worshipped as a deliverer. But the stern duty of defence stared him in the face, and to the disposi- tion of 'his forces he quickly applied himself. The gates were hardly closed and securell fastened when the redskins began to rally, and soon their menaces made it apparent we were threatened with a siege by a body of treacherous foes numbering, at least; five to our one. Seeing this I nerved my arm to the battle, and, doffing nal hat, said reverently: "The Lord preserve us!" (To be eostinued.)

,•CA.PITAT." CHAT.

It is difficult to get at the reasons which have induced the inhabitants of St. Pancras to reject the adoption of the Public Libraries Act by a majority ot 3429. Since 1894, when the last poll was taken on the same question, the adverse majorits has more than doubled. In another large metropolitan parish, that of Marylebon.eatie anti-library majority has also prevailed, but in the latter case it shrank from 4263 in 1895 to 376 in the present year. But in St. Pancras the party of enlightenment brought forward all its forces, which were not much more than in 1894. On the other hand, the anti-library party has increased by over 2900. The latter party has been working hard for months wherever the library party was badly organised. But the value of a library is a point that required no argument in support of it, and the reason must therefore be f sought outside the canvassing. The parish is full of small shopkeeP hatvhiirs heavily rented, and, therefore, enengselyee a desperate struggle to live, to read. Most of them ahnigdh highly rated, s probably object to pay for a supply of novels for young women with little or nothing to do. The Lord Mayor and Corporation intend to hon- our the Sirclar upon a scale of great brilliancy. There will be two events —the presentation of the freedom of the City,and of a sword of honour in the afternoon at the Guildhall, and a state dinner at the Mansion house in the evening. For the former about 2;000 invitations will be issued. At the evening banquet, such officers as took part in the Soudan operations who may have returned to Lon- don will be present, and in other respects the capa- cious dining hall will be filled with official guests. The Lord Mayor is inundated with applications for aneinvitation, and most of the applicants have to accept the humi:iation of a polite refusal, grounded on the plea of want of room. Owing to the Fashoda complication attention has been withdrawn from the attitude of the Khedive in relation to the Khartoum success, although I understand it is a cause of coasiderable anxiety at the Foreign Office. It may be remem- bered that the first congratulatory telegram that reached the British agency at Cairo was from the Emperor William, and that similar messages from Her Majesty and Lord Wolseley quickly followed. On the news of the victory becoming more widely known congratulations poured in from all parts. The Khedive might naturally have been expected to sand cordial greetings to the Sirdar, being more directly interested than anyone else in the result of the battle. His reticence at such a moment was decidedly equivocal; and even more remarkable was his absence on General Kitchener's return to Cairo. The conduct of Abbas is another sign of revolt egeinst the cenditions which secured his father Towfik on the Khedival throne, and which alone can keep Abbas, and his dynasty there. A few years ago the Khedive dismissed his Ministers right and left and appointed others,in open defiance of Lord Cromer. His present action goes far to create a very difficult and menacing position. At a similar moment of friction Lord Granville wrote to Lord Cromer—" It should be made clear to the Esypti an Ministers and governors of provinces that ' the responsibility which for the time rests on England obliges Her Majesty's Government to insist 'on the adoption of the policy which they recom- mend, and that it will be necessary that those Ministers and governors who do not follow this course should cease to hold their offices." Mr. Edward Hamilton, whose death was recently announced, was, 1 find, Mr. Gladstone's exact. con- temporary at Eton. They entered the school on the same day. When Mr. George Russell was writing his monograph on Mr. Gladstone in the series of" The Queen's Prime Ministers" he deter- mined to make a special study of the Eron days, which previous biograpb ere had mostly ignored. The difficulty was that though some of Mr. Gladstene's predecessors at Eton were still alive, his own gene- ration had almost entirely perished. In this diffi- culty Sir Edward Hamilton, of the Treasury (son of that Bishop of Salisbury who expressed such deep obligations to Mr. Gledstone'e influence on him in his schooldays) recommended Mr. Ressell to apply to his uncle, Mr. Hamilton. of Charters. This was done, and Mr. Hamilton. whoee memory was re- markably exact, furnished the greater part of that account of the Eton school-days which formed the- best part of Mr. Russell's book. Out of these recol lections one curious point of controversy arose. Mr. Hamilton said that young Gladstone, though he was fond of rowing, took no part in school games, and this statement duly appeared in the mono- graph. When it came to Mr. Glacletene's know- ledge be was highly indignant, declared that he had played both cricket and football and was in the second eleven at cricket, and desired that the erroneous statement might he correct el in a later edieion, which was duly done. The very circumstantial story published a few days ago, that the Abyssinian Emperor had made over a coaling station on the Red Sea to Russia, is not credited in military and navel circles here. At all events, it create no alarm in any coateer. Accord- log to that story, an old right of Alres-inia to some noeition on the Red Sea coast Was raked out of ob- livion, but nobody seems to know what Ahvaainien ruler ever owned a strip of the Eel Sea (neat in the neighbourhood of Zsila and Obnir. The gory is n revival of earlier rumours of a similar kind which had ne foundation. It is more probably an in- ference from Russia's desire for a Red Sea coaling station. Rumour asserted that Russia coveted Ra- heita, on the Red Sea littoral, as a coaling station end it is the same place that is no TV by gossip as- signed to her. Raheita, since 1861. ha e been under the protection of Italy. who on the previous occa- sions invariably warned Russia off. Previous te 1861 it was in the possession nf Egypt. en that Menelik must go a long way back to foand an Abyssinian claim. Russ*a will scarcely raise a question with Italy as to the possession of tbe station, which in time of peace can hardly be needed for commercial purposes, and in time of war could be of no use. . _

He hes already retunned to this country. and the War Office lost no time in sum- moninir him to a conference. in order that it mil,-.,ht ascertain from him his official ()Pinion on their effi- cacy, He was in consultation with the military authorities for a considerable time, and I holi.vA it is their intention to nrnceed with the manufacture of the shell unnn an nxtensive scale. The accounts of its nsrformances in the Soudan which are current in the service clubs have fired the imagination of old campaigners, who will hear when Parliament meets of the intentions of the Government with reference to its manufacture and use. The London County Council nt its mestinct on Tuesday decided to complete the vurchnse of the South London tramways for £850.000. About 2 1-8 miles of line are already in roaqesoirm or the Coun- cil. Starting on the Ist January, 1899. the Council intends to work the tramways itsslf. " SOCIALISM AT WOREC."

On Thursday last week at the Cairo-street school,Mr.F. Brocklehurst, 8.A., (Manchester City Council),delivered lecture on the above subject. There was a fair atten- dance. Mr. J. Podmore junior occupied the chair and introduced the Lecturer to the company. The Lecruasn said it was four years since be had lectured in Warrington and during that time he had travelled a great deal, and had had many experiences. He had seen experiments of what people called Socialist Colonies, but they were not true experiments. They did more harm than good to Socialism. What were they ? Nothing but combinations of men and women who despaired of society. Socialism meant a transformation of the whole of society and that transformation could only be done by men living a social life. Socialism was very old and began practically when two people agreed to dwell and live together. Wherever men combined over anything, that was socialism.. Take for example the administration of justice In matters of wrong-doing. Socialism was there represented. Under the old law of Goel it was a man's most solemn duty to kill his brother's murderer. But now society bad limited the scope of individuals, and said " we must do for you what you originally did for yourself." What was originally a duty had become crime. The fundamental principle underlying the change was socialism. The change had taken place gradually, and so socialism would advance gradually. Socialism would one day make it unnecessary for a man to fight with his brother for daily bread. Moral obliga- tion had been taken from rnan, and toms day material obligations would also be taken from him. Those socie- ties which had beeome more socialistic had become more civilized. There was so much socialism in society that man failed to observe it. The formation of limited industrial concerns was only one of the tendencies towards socialism. They bad seen how socialist tenden- cies had developed within the last few years- Socialism was inevitable. It was the next stage in industrial life. If they would take any municipality iu this country, they would find it was honeycombad with socialism. Our streets were part of a socialistic plan, and one day land would be the same. Socialism was shewn in all those municipal properties such as the trams, water, lighting, libraries, parks, and bands, ec. They were all for the common good whether one used them or not. In fact there was a general tendency in the public life towards socialism, and they were wife men who recog- dised its force. Men often thought they were opposing socialism when they were going in for the municipalisa- tion of train services. Socialism had been created by no man, it had simply been interpreted. Socialism was simply a social evolution. The goal of true socialism, was fraternal endeavour.

A REMARKABLE SIORY.

The following facts were obtained at first hand and are wholly accurate and trustworthy. The story has no likeness to the numerous wonder tales Which have no better foundation than gossip and imagination. The occurrence, should it prove to be true, was deemed of se great importance that a special messenger was detailed to visit the spot with instructions to inquire carefully and thoroughly into every point of the case ; with the understanding that if his investigation bore out the statements earliest made, a sufficiently full account was Ito be published for the information, and benefit of the public. His report being clear and satisfactory, a con- densed summary of it is here given : It seems that a respectable family named Skinner re- sides at 34, High Stret,Banbury, Oxon. In the summer of 1895 a son, William Skinner, now about eighteen years old, met with an actident —a heavy fall, such as expected he would soon Y no bones were bmriogkhetahaanpdp ehni st for iaennydos nne t uFr aolrl tyuna ts I get over it. But he did not. Instead of recovering is strength he became weak and irritable. Although there was no injury calling for surgical interference, the lad's system appeared to be profoundly shikei. His appetite, previously robust, as with all healthy fellows of his age, grew poor and fitful. The attempt to build him up on food was consequently a failure. The liquid food, of which he took a limited amount, bad no effect. He gained no strength from it. In spite of medical treat- ' meat young Skinner lost flesh at a frightful rate, his bones thre 'toning t) protru le thrall gh the skin. alarming. He was conetantly twitch hie and totally un- able to sit still or to remain quiet. Bad as his experi- ence was by day he was al wave worse at nig et. He obtained so little material sleep that the doetor was (") - liged to administer sleeeing draughts—a course which no medical man adopts when it can be avoided. When awake, so great was the poor boy's nervousness, that be could not endure to be approached or sookea to. The very sight of him is sail by the neighbours to have be n a pitiable one to witness. People who caged to see him out of sympathy and to offer any services they might be able to render; wore of one mind ; they said he could not possibly recover. Looking upon his son's deplorable and apparently hopeless sta'e his broken-hearted mother said, " I pray that God may take him,"

Yet merciful death did not corns to his relief. For over two years he lingered on, sometimes a trifle better, as was believed, and then relapsing into his former con- dit:on. The local doctor diagnosed the disease as St. Vitus' dance, and treated the patient for several months en that theory of the case ; unhappily without rood re- sult. In view of obtaining the highest medical opinion of this mysterious affliction the lad was then taken for examination to the National Hospital,Lonion,and after- wards admitted to the Banbury Hospital, where he rea mained three months, with no material improvement ; the physicians' judgment being that there was no hope for him. Thus nearly a year went by.

In May, 1896, his indefatigable mother—who fought for her offspring as only mothers will—on a letter of re- commendation took the poor striken boy to the Con- valescent Home at Eastbourne, and then again to the National Hcspital in London, with no better outcome than before. On his return home William Skinner was to all appearance a complete wreck, his mind being quite unhinged. He was tenderly replaced in bed, as much dead as alive, and there remained, virtually a breathing corpse, month after month, season after season. His inherent vitality, has unconscious bold on life, surprised all who saw him. But plainly, the end could not be much longer delayed. Events now bring us to September, 1897, when the unexpected happened, as it so often does. frisnd of the family, Mrs. Jewell of Sibford, called. In conver- sation on the bey's illness she urged the immediate use of a remedy known as Mother gel what it had done for her husband at a time when other Sei's Syrup, telling treatment was unavailing. On this advice Mrs. Skinner acted; taking responsibilities upon herself. In a short time—when the sufferer's desperate state is considered —there was a marked improvement. Ho emit eat with same relish, the food causing no distress or reaction. Other medicines were entirely laid aside and depen- dence placed on this one alone : but the result justifieo 83 this seemingly rash prccoiding. Little by little the lad gained r thestrength gve climbing, to speak, from the verge As his bsdil grow stronger his mind cleared and brightened. T., the amazement of friends ra ; o and physicians in two months' time he was able to leave the bOUSO and go about. At the date of this writing, rs February 4, 1898, William Skinner is in r)bust health, and thehis is but a all past two yea mother insists that his life wf nsast. f ag mmpo savedin terribleb yeorthreir. i esn 9 Hisei Ig e: otSyrup, and nobody of the jusuy who followed the case from the start is ins'inel to dispute her opinion.

WARRINGTON WOMEN'S LIBERAL

ASSOCIATION.

ANNUAL MEETING.

- -- The fourth annual meeting of thii Association took place at the Liberal Club on Monday evening. There Was a good attendance.the meeting being preceded by as tea, after which Mrs. Ziba Armitage took the chair be- ing supported by Mrs. H. Roberts (treasurer) and Miss Bessie Dutton. Mrs. ARMITAGE expressed her regret at the absence of Mrs. Grimshaw, their secretary, Mrs. Rodger and Mrs. Adounfield. Mrs. Grimshaw and Mrs. Mounfleld had been ill, but they all hoped they would soon be better. Mrs. Walker also was unavoidably absent. Yet although they had a good attendance they missed many oftheir friend whom they were accustomed to see at those meetings. She would like to say a word about the organization of the Women's Libsral Federation whose headquarters were in London. Delegates from all the associations throughout the country went up there each year. This year Mrs. Rodger and Adios Dutton attended on behalf of the Warrington Association. Coming nearer home there was the Lancashire and Cheshire Women's Liberal Asso- ciation, with headquarters in Manchester, and the Federal Council of the Lancashire and Cheshire Women's Libsral Associations, with headquarters in Liverpool. They had been affiliated to both these, but now they had decided to belong only to the Liverpool Association, because the Manchester Association had gone in very strongly for making women's suffrage a test question with all the Parliamentary candidates. They refused to work for a Liberal candidate for Parliamen- tary honours who would not vote for women's suffrage. They in Warrington felt that they must work for the Liberal candidate whether he believed in wonsen's suff- rage or not. (Hear, hear.) At a meeting at Liverpool a little while ago it was decided to hold a conference at Blackburn, and to thoroughly talk over this test ques- tion. There was a good deal of heart-burning over the question, both in Manchester and Liverpool, and she hoped EOM satisfactory conclusion would be arrived at. (Applause.) Mrs. Grimshaw as secretary should have read her rapore that evening, and in her absence she (the president), would read it.

THE REPORT. We opened our session on October let, 1897, by a tea meeting at the Liberal Club, at which Mrs. Solly, of Cape Colony,gave a very interesting address on" South African Politics." There were not so many members present as we could have wished, being an incoaveaient evening (Friday), but three new members were enrolled, whi± was very gratifying. On October 15th lilies Bal. garnie gave an address en " Women of the Victorian Age." The meeting was fairly well attended, but again we were obliged to have it on the unfortunate Friday. We had a very encouraging meeting on Monday,Decem- her 13th, when Mrs. 4tswart Brown gave a very clear and interesting address 6n current patios. The attend- ance was good, and a general interest was awakened. In January the annual ball was held, which was well. attended and well-managed. The summer picnic also was a decided success. Mrs. Armitage added that there had been an increase of 30 in the membership during the year. (Applause.) Mrs. ROBERTS (treasurer) submitted the annual state- ment of accounts, which shewed that the year was com- menced with a balance in hand of 12s. 611., and at the bankers of £2O Os. 2d. Items of income brought the total receipts to £53 7s. 4. At the close of the year there was a balence in haul of £1 6s. Bid., and cash at the bank £l7 155. sd. The reports were adopted. Miss DUTTON said she rejoiced that they had been in existence now about seven years, and they were still living and thought they were flourishing. They had had 30 new membets lately. Sometimes people told them they were dead, but they knew they lived and it was very awkward. (Laughter). Mrs. Rodger and she derived much:pleasure and profit in attending as dele- gates of the Warringeem branch the Federation meetings in London. Severe years ago Lady Carlisle came to their great Liberal bazaar, and she was instrumental in having formed a Womea's Liberal Association in Warrington. As president of the year she presided at the London meetings, and they were very pleased to see her. She made a splendid opening speech. practical, real, and helpful. She moved the adoption of the annual report. Then Mrs. Eva McLaren, the treasurer, a lady well-known and beloved in that association, pleaded tor increased funds. rhe qv estien afterwards arose as to the pay- ment of public sneakers, and still retain their seat on the ex,cutive. It was considered to be reasonable that they ehould be paid and not lose their seat Oa the execu tive, and a motion to that effect was carried. Then there were motione on some Rule and the Disestablish- merit of the Weiss Church. They all felt that Ireland should have the direction of its own taxation and ether matters, and they had no hesitation in voting for Hema Rule. They also felt that Wales should not have a church which was in an anomalous position.and they voted for disestablishment. Afterwards come a motion with re- ference to women as ceunty councillors. They had woman on boards of guardians and parish councils. There was one lady present in the room whom they were very glad was on the Board of Guardians for Warrington. In Warrineton they had done a vary good work. (Ap- plause.) The meeting thought it desirable that women should have an opportunity of sitting on county coun- cils. There wer3 still doors open. and if they kept peg- ging away at the question soiree result was sure to fol- low. Then came the knotty question of women's suffrage. They knew what a burning question that was. Mrs. Price Hughes made a vigorous spnech for it and Mrs. Stewart Brown also. Both were very convincing. Everyone voted for a motion in favour of women's suf- frage. It was proposed that they should not vote for a candidate who was not in favour of women's auffrege. A great many said they had no right to help a candidate who did not help them,and that provoked'a great variety of opinion. They from Warringtoa considered it should not be regarded as a test question, because if it WAS women Liberals would be practically working for the Conservatives CO cases where the Libnral did not favour women's suffrage. The majority were against it being regarded as a test question. Toe minority asked for a division, and on the vote it was found that 171 were on one side and four or five hundred on the other. The Indian Frontier ques- tion was afterwards discussed, and then came the far more interesting question to them, of prison reform. The feeling of the meeting was that a reform iii the management and conduct of prisons was desirable. Another interesting philanthropic question was the labour question as it affected women, and it was consi- dered by the meeting that legislatioa with regard to the dangerous trades was necessary. On the subject of vaccination a motion was passed to the effect that it should not be compulsory where people had a conscien- tious objoction. A discussion also took place on the temperance question, and a motion for local veto was unanimously adapted. (Applause.) Mies GORN&LL tastefully sang a couple of songs, Miss Walker accompanying. Mr. Mounfleld proposed a vote of thanks to Miss Dutton and the Misses Gomel' and Walker.—Mr. F. 11. Roberts seconded, and the motion was carried with enthusiasm.—Miss Dutton having re- plied, the meeting was brought to a close by the singing of the National Anthem.

A WARRINGTON SOLDIER AT

===== 0002601_18981014_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
OMDURMAN.

BATTLE EXPERIENCES.

Mr. E. Sykes, of 25' Willis-street, Manchester- road, has received the following interesting letter from his son, Pioneer Edward Sykes, of the Grena- dier Guards who took part in the great battle at Omdurman. The letter reals as follows ; exclu- sive of person matter not for publication : Omdurman, September 6th 189 S.

"Safe and sound as ever after the battle I I need not tire you by telling you about the march here, as it was a very trying one. We left Albara camp and proceeded to Khartoum. All went well until about August 30th when we first came in touch witb the enemy's outposts and forced them to retire. On September Ist we caught sight of the whole of the Khalifa's army lying in trenches, and waiting for us to attack them, which our commander would not do as he wanted to get his whole force to- gether. We had to keep awake all night, waiting, ready if they attacked us.At last,raorning came,and about 6 a.m. we could see them coming about 3 miles off. There were 33,000 of them, armed with spears,svvords, and daggers, As they rushed towards us they yelled their war-cry, which sent a thrill through us. When we got about 2,700 yards from them we opened fire and then a squadron of their horsemen charged us, but we shot them down to a man, and almost all the horses dashed away riderless. We continued firing and at last our artillery smt shells at them, and you could see them scattering in all directions. 'Twas a sight never to be forgotten I They then tried to attack our left, but the Sou lanese fought like bricks and sent the Dervishes back. They then retreated to the hills and we followed them, and after seven hours' and a half hard fighting we retired to oar camp, having scattered them in all directions, and having won a hard fight. saw some terrible sights. One man had his arm blown off, and he trisd to stab one of our fellows who was giving him a drink. Another fellow ran him through and through with a bayonet. The day after the battle we went oat countihg the bodies of the Dervishes who were killed, and four battalions who were out counted about 20,000. Our Regiment was very lucky, not one man being killed, though we had a few wounded, I think we leave here to go down the Nile and then to England to-morrow. I shall be home again before Christmas, so for the present I will conclude, etc.—Tun.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Council was held at the Bewsey- street Chambers, on Wednesday afternoon with Mr. Josiah Ball (in the chair). There were also preterit Messrs. Henry Thornton, J. W. Knowles, Thomas Hal- to., W. Houghton, J. Johnson, Forshaw, A. Brookes, with Dr Sephton (medical officer, Mr. F. Kerbot, , (inventor), and Mr. D. S. Stone, for Mr. W. Owen (surveyor). THE LIGHTING OF PADGATE AND FEARNHEAD. The question of adlitlcual lighting facilities for Pad- gate and Fearnhmi came up for cousideratioa. After some iscussion of the number of extra lamps required the chairman moved and Mr. Knowles seconded that a special committet, be formed for the purpose cf fixing on the sites and the number. The following committee was appointed to deal with the mater: Mews. Halton, Houghton, Thornton, Brookes and Knowles. Mr. HOUGHTON : I think either this committee Or this Council ought to approach the Gas Committee of the Warrington Corporatiln in respect to the price of gas and lamps. Since we initiated the movement they have reduced the price to the consumers, and public lighting should have some advantage too.—This was agreed to.

THE REV. J. YONGE AND "THE GREATEST SHOW ON EARTH."

The subject of the 'Rev. J. Yongee discourse in , Wycliffe Congregational Chureh. Warrington, on Sun- day evening was" The Greatest Show on Eirth." He said the text was the event that occurred in the town the previous day. The warrant for the discourse wan ' in the fact that the whole community was moved by ' this visit of "The Greatest Show on Earth." SOME,. times it had been cast as a reproach against the pulpit that it concerned itself so little with current events and common affairs. In so far as that reproach was truee lit was also just. The pulpit,inde d,was not meant to be the place for the constant discussion of the eve eta of day; for it b.d its own definite and Divinely appointed theme, to which its unswerving loyalty was supremely due. Still, when a passing lent itself to illustrate and enforce the -- sage of the pulpit, then the pulpit missed its opportunity if it let the event pass without pressing it into service. Possibly in the case before them some might think that it was beneath the dignity of the pul- pit to take notice of the passing of a show even though it might claim to be" the greatest show on earth." The answer was that nothine ceuld b 3 beneath the dignity of the pulpit that strongly appeals to the imagination, and stirred tbe interests of the people. The interest and excitement of the population on Saturday, were an un- mistakeable display of a preat feature of human nature. In every human being.—rnan. woman, and child,—the leve of the spectacular was absolutely netural. Now this taste for the spectacular being thus stroae, and universal, it was no m etter of surprise that the visit on Satutd ty of " the greatest show on earth" should have interested and exci- ted Warrington as nothing perhaps ever did before. The th aught would come to any reflective rand as to whether the huge and stupendous enterprise of sen- sation entettainment was not a alga of the times, that was no, altogether at any rate of the best omen. "The greatest snow on earth " sal its unrivalled su-ceas re- minded them of the fact that they lived in what seemed to be growingly, a pleasure loving, sensation seeking, excitement craving age. No one who carefully observed the tendency of the day could doubt the accuracy of that s'atertent. He was not de:stir ens of having a tilt aeainst entertainments and recreations as such, because to do so would not only be unpopular, but it would be foolish and wrong. Hard and honest werkers sheuld have their periods of relaxation. Weartel minds and worried hearts should have opportunities of wholesome and pleasureable . relief. But, what seemed to be a growing tendency of the day—among the younger people especially—was the tendency to fill up the most of their leisure hours with same form or other of more or less exciting recreation. That the taste existed was testified to by every theatrie cal placard whick they c )uld see on the public hoard- ings. Sensationalism was the order of the day, and nothing would " take" unlees it had about it some- thing of the appearance and spice of a sensation. He would not say one were] of censure concerning the char- eater rf the performances, which although in the town on Saturday, he personally had not been yet able to wit- ness. His present observation was strictly and solely this that in so far as that famous and highly sensational entertainment was an evidence of an undue craving for sensationaliem, and excitement in pleasure seeking, it was suggestive of serious caution. His impression in regard to the shew which was in the town on Saturday was that it was vastly superior to the miserable and do- grading sees itionaliam frequently provided upon the ' boards of theatres of VIA town. If he in saying that I judged some of those performances wrongly, than their ' own advertisements and pictures were responsible for I his mistake. Be was not sure whether public municipal i functionaries should roe suppress those pictures of as- sault and murder that were the frequent disgrace of Sankey-street and Rylands.street. It was to him a source of painful thought that such plays were seen by young people, But did they pot all come of the demand there was for sensetion, e xeitement ? It was a huge mistake to imanine that nothing could be enjoyable that did not excite. l',‘r plc could not live upon exeite- moot any more than they could have peed health by an exclusive diet of sweetmeate. Speaking of the lar,e 'mount of money which wouli be spent to view the sheivr in Saturday, he poieted out that more money as well as time was trtent upon amusoment and re.oreation than multitudes of people could preperly afford. He believed that to be true as a mere matter of personal economy and thrift, which were motives that ought to i afluenCe the .condult even of people who made no religious pro- fession. Possibly some of them might have paid hand. comely for a seat t o see t 11.4 show, and who would re- spond to the appeal, say, of foreign miesione, with the smallest coin. If they did, forgive him frankly saying, that as disciples of the Master, they did wrong. He then spoke upon, the transience, the evanescence, the swift and utter flight of mere temueement• Tnere was nothing so ephemeral,an fleeting, so f ading,as pleasure ! The preacher proceeded tie speak upon the supreme spec- tacle of Calvary—that spectacle of lore. The last book in the bible seemed ta say that there was yet to be one other stupendous spectacle upon which the human race would be summoned to gaze. It was the scene of the Judgment. It was a aeene that would be impossible of avoidanc- ! They might evaim Calvary. But they would not be able to evade the Gnat White Throne! For all would be there,and " every eve should see Him." That would be the transcendent climax of human des. tiny, or the transcendent decree of human doom.
