THE EXAMINEE.

OCTOBER 14th 1898.

IT is significant of the tension The Channel which exists that an ordinary Fleet.

autumnal cruise of the Channel Fleet should excite some warlike apprehensions. It is customary for the Fleet to make a short cruise at this time of the year, and to return in time for Christmas. With but small variation, , this plan has been pursued for some time, and no one has seen anything alarming in the fact. The order for the present cruise was given some time ago. It was to have commenced this week, but some of the vessels cannot be got ready at the time appointed, and there will be a little delay in consequence. The delay is not being regarded as so significant as the intimation that the ships are to take with them a six months supply of ammu- nition,One account put this as"six months' stores," which is quite another matter as it is not intended that the Fleet shall be away for so long a period. , The ammunition account is probably the correct one,and the true explanation may be found in the desire of the Admiralty to pay a little more atten- tion to gun-practice, concerning which some un- complimentary remarks have been made in various quarters. The Fleet is intended to visit A.rosa Bay, Vigo Bay, and Gibraltar, returning to Ports- mouth some time about December 16th, when the crews will proceed to take their periods of Christ- mas leave ashore. These appear to be the true facts of the case, and it is important they should be stated,because the sensationalisins current have no larger basis of evidence accessible to the public at large. There can be nothing of a minatory character in sending the Fleet on its customary cruise, especially as some of its operations have been curtailed already, partly because of the trouble in the South Wales coal trade, and partly out of a desire not to offend the susceptibilities of France. The French people will not see in the despatch of the Channel Float to the Mediter- ranean any suspicious intentions, if we do not magnify a simple seasonal operation for the pur- pose of bluster and brag. The Fleet must receive its customary training, whether our foreign rela- tions are pacific or unsettled. The orders sent to Portsmouth respecting it have no connection with the Fashoda dispute, and are not designed to create any wild presumptions in that direction. It is 'probable that if there were a situation in which the Channel Fleet j were going to play a part it would not be sent to Gibraltar at all, but detained at home for its more legitimate service. The nervous condition of the public mind, more- over, is simply aggravated by these:constant inter- pretations of the ordinary practices of the Admi- ralty as meaning dreadful things. Our neighbours are not likely to be alarmed by suspicions which are in the main confined to a very liraited range at home. They accept our declarations, they are not moved by cruises and training operations,and it seems to us that it is the duty of public journals and public men to refrain from whatever is calcu- lated to foster alarms and intensify legitimate anxieties.

THE disposition to whip up South African Affairs, excitement about affairs at the Cape is manifest, to what- ever cause it may be attributed, but it is not meeting with much success. There are no large issues at stake, as we had Wen led to believe, as far as the affairs of Capo Colony are concerned, and elsewher the range for speculation is more

personal than practical. The so-called crisis at the Cape is a very small affair. A vote of no con-

Hence against the Sprigg Ministry has been carried by two votes after a debate in which only two speakers took part, and which lasted less than an hour. The Government is intending to ignore the vote, and get on with business. There has ; been a promise on the part of two a,6 three Bond members to assist in voting Supply, and though there may be blocking tactics, probably the votes will be got through,

and the practical questions to be settled will be approached. The Redistribution Bill must be passed, whichever party is in power, and as SOOll as it has been disposed of other elections must take place. There seems to be no real differ- ence of opinion as to British supremacy between the Bond and the Progressive members, and if the statement to the contrary has failed to rally sufficient support to Sir GORDON SPRIGG and MT. RHODES to give them a real working majority,wo may regard the situation with calmness. Local and not Imperial questions remain for considera- tion, and they do not seem likely to be of great interest for us at home. Mr. RHODES has been beaten for the time being, but he has doubtless many cards up his sleeve to play at the right moment. He is far too astute a man to submit to a rebuff which leaves him by no means shorn of influence and position. There are some unruly spirits in the Bond party who will talk treason and assail Sir A. MILNER, ard threaten desperate things. Possibly PRESIDENT KRUGER will be content to avoid any conflict with Cape Colony or Rhodesia on matters of a railway and tariff character for the present. The ono question which interests him as keenly as it does us is that of Delagoa By and the future of the railway. The award is expected during the present year, as the Commissioner who had I)eeti ill is now restored to health and able to co-operate in drawing up the final terms of the award. The result is a foregone conclusion, and the award will be against Portugal; and Portugal will be unable to meet it, and have to resort to a compromise, ia which British interests will be advantaged. Mr. Ruonns will then play one of his cards, though we cannot quite say which it will be. In any event the Transvaal will have to be more subdued than it ha 3 been since the raid, and the Orange Frge State will probably see the wisdom of holding itself free from the entanglements which PRESIDENT Kuroan has Leen adroitly preparing font.

The police call upon the public for aid so sel- dom that no one can defend men from whom as- sistance has been vainly demanded in a street scene which might have ended in serious injuries being inflicted upon the constable. In the Mail- ing case the three persons who refused their aid to the police may have done so in ignorance of the law. But, apart from this particular instance, ignorance of the law cannot be received as s sound plea to avert sentence, though it might fairly tend towards mitigation in the usual penal- ties. It is a pity, perhaps, that policemen in dan- gerous quarters do not call for the aid of the passers by more often. Such action would at any rate familiarise the public with a law which seeinS to be but imperfectly understood. Here, how- ever, the traditional British pride and love of in- dependence come-into play. Not more that half- a-dozen times a year do policemen commandeer the public "in the Queen's name," but cases of assault are constantly occurring in which the police would be perfectly justified in so doing. Nor would good citizens object to this tax upon their strength and their time. The police are en- gaged in duties wholly concerned with the welfare of the public, and it would be disgraceful to sug- gest that bystanders may keep aloof while con- stables are being worsted in the struggle with savage ruffianism.

In a week or two the Municipal Elections in Warrington will have taken place and nine. members of the Council will then have been either .re-elected or defeated. This year six Liberal Councillors retire and three Con- servatives. Seeing that there is a preponderance of Liberals retiring it is very unlikelY that we shall hear anything about a compromise being made between the two parties. These cow venient arrangements for the benefit of retiring- 1 Councillors are generally proposed when there is S decided majority of Conservative members seeking re-election. But we do not think the Liberals have anything to fear from an all-round contest. The six Liberals who will again seek the suffrages of the electors are all " good men and true." They are: Councillor Monks in Whitecross Ward, Councillor Wilkinson in Orford Ward, Councillor Furness in St. John's Ward, Councillor Ellison in Latchford Ward, Councillor Burton in St. Austin' Ward, and Councillor Duncan in Fairfield Ward. Their chances of success are, in our opiniont superior tothose of the three retiring Conservatives —Councillors Rogers,Heaton and Cannel!, none of whom have distinguished themselves in the ratepayers' service. Three years ago Councillor, Rogers, who is connected with "the trade, succeeded in gaining a seat on the Council for Bewsey VV ard as an " independent " candidate. Since then the electors of Bewsey Ward have had an opportunity of testing his "independency," which seems to have consisted of a meek following of the Conservative leaders, and we may anticipate that they will dispense with his:services and give prefer- ence to a representative working than like Mr.T.D Malkin. Councillor Heaton when opposed by Clay in Town Hall Ward succeeded in retaining his seat by four votes. His chances of success op the present occasion cannot therefore be consi- dered very rosy. With reference to Councillor Cannell's candidature, the electors will no doid?t be glad of the opportunity of expressing their appreciation or otherwise of his services in the Council Chamber.

--- We are requested by the Mayer Of Warrington to draw the attention of our readers to the Fund which is being raised by the Lord Mayor at the Mansion Howe for the relief of the widespread an : cute distress occasioned by the recent terrible hurricane and tidal wave in the West Indies, which has entail enormous suffering among the population of tli° Islands of Barbados, St. Vincent and St. Lucia' The Lord Mayor in his appeal states that " thee,e Colonies have been, and still are, passing throne a period of intense financial depression and they are absolutely helpless to render commensurate or even temporary aid in this great calamity."

Father Gore, who was accorded hearty cheers upon their return to Runcorn. finutawrar MOTETAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS. The following letter was received from the Local Government Board :—Dear Sir, lam directed by the Local Government Board to acknowli-d 70 the receot of your letter of tne 20th ult, with reference to the applica- tion of the Runcorn Rural District Council for the Board's sanction to the payment of the sum of £3 Ss to the Parochial Nurse Society of Frodsham, in regret of disinfectants provided by the society during the epidemic of scarlet fever at Frodsham last year. I am to advert to your letter of the 19th August, 1898,1 and to inquire how the question of providing hospltal accommodation for the infectious disease other than small-pea now stands. lam at the same time to point out that so serious an epidemic, which necessitated the closing of schools,should in accordance with the beard's order have been reported to Grm at the time by the Medical Officer of Health. lam your obedient servant ALFRED D. A letter was received from Miss Isabel G. Lowndes, secretary of the Northern Workhouse Nursing Associa- tion, stating that she regretted that any mis-understand- ing should hays arisen regarding the length of Nurse Willis' appointment. The nurse had frequently asked for another appointment with a higher salary chiefly I from family reasons, and that was the sole reason for transferring her elsewhere without any thought of in- juring the interests of the Guarclans in any way what- ever. She further stated that the Association were prepared to send Nurse Annie Todd,aged 29 years,trained at Sheffield. She would however require a higher salary I than £25, as her present salary was £23 per annum I with £2 for uniform. If the Guardiaas desired the ap- pointment could in future be made for two years. Mr. A. R. Norman, chairman of the House Committee, stated that they had never had the slightest intimation that the nurses were dissatisfied. Not long ago they fixed their salary at a certain figure and arranged to give them an annual advaucc of £1 until a maximum of £3O por annum was reached. It seemed to them that the Association were not dealing fairly. They were simply going to take th,dr nurse away, and the nurse they suggested should take her place was already receiv- ing £2B, and would probably want an advance on that. The feeling of the committee was very strongly against the way in which the Association condunted their busi- ness. They ought to have acquainted ttin board if there was any complaint and given them an opportunity, if they thought fit,of giving the present nurse an increased salary. The nurse now told them that had she thought the Board would have advanced her salary she would have asked for an incresse. The Chairman explained that the commencing salary was £25, and was progreqsive at the rate of per annum until a maximum of £3O was reached; Admiral Jones-Parry moved that a letter be sent to the Association that in future no letter would be taken notice of unless it had previously been sent to the Board. It seemed a most extraordinary thing to write to the nurse and ask her to send in her resignation without first of all acquainting the Beard. Mr. Talbot seconded and the motion was carried. It was also decided to ask the Association if thy would guarantee in future to supply nurses on the understanding that they remained two years when ap- pointed by the guardians. TSB LAM MIL YARWC9D,

BOY DROWNED AT RUNCORN DOCK.

FUTILE AT TEMPT AT RESUSCITATION.

At the Runcorn Police Court, on Monday morning. an inquest was held by Mr. J. E. ‘l,..riley, coroner, teach- ing the tieath a a little boy, Thomas Arthur Wood, aged five who was drowned on Saturday whilst playing in the vicinity of the Frances Dock, Runcorn.—Peter Woods' labourer, of Peicival-lane, ideatified the body as hat of his son Thomas Arthur, whom he saw last alive lOn Saturday about 1.30. At about 4.30, he he .rd that the boy had fallen iuto the dock. He saw the body of his son taken out of the water.—Abraham Colclough, captain of the " Tartar," lying in the Frances 3 Dock, Runcorn, said on Saturday evening, about 4.30, he was in his cabin,when he heard a noise outside. Ha went out and was told by two young men that a boy had fallen into the water. He got a shaft,and putting it down the side of the dock fished out the b Ay. Immediately, the young men and himself tried to resuscitate the body; but their efforts were of no avail. One of the young men present was fully acquainted with the method, and told them what to do. Finally the body was taken borne. Witness thought the boy had been in the wet a. about 10 minutes toe quarter of an tour.—Thomas Hughes,6-year old son of Mr. David Hughes, Percivat's-lane, Runcorn, stat,d that the deceased was pulling a rope, when he suddenly slipped and fell into the water and sank. Witness was so frightened that he went home and told his mother—Police Constable Wilson, said, from infor- mation received, he went to the Frances Dock about 20 minutes to five. Be found Captain Colclough and two young tnen—one named John Gatley—trying to resusci- tate the body of the deceased. Witness assisted them for about 20 minutes, but to no purpose.—The Coroner colsidered tnere was no doubt but that the boy was accidentally drowned. Be thought, however, that the men deserved every praise for their conduct in the attempt to resuscitate the body; it was a worthy effort to save life.—The jury returned a verdict of " Acciden- drowned."

DEATH OF MR. 8-AMU', YARWOOD.

Mr. Samuel Yarwood, the well-known and respected guardian for Newton Ward, and who for the last 50 years has carried on an extensive business as cab pro- prietor, of Albert-street, Runcorn, died very suddenly on Saturday evening. On the Saturday, Mr. Yar wood, though in his 74th year, attended business in town with his accustomed regularity, and oven spoke of fulfilling his engagements for the forthcoming week—the Board of Guardians meeting, and a meeting of the Board of Directors of the Widnes, Runcorn and District Billpost- ing Company, of which he was a member. Returning home he complained of feeling ill at about ten - minutes past seven on Saturday', and notwithstanding medical attendance, he died at about five minutes past nine the same night. Previous to founding his business as a cab proprietor Mr. Yarwood was a well known auctioneer. What makes the circumstances attending Mr. Yardwood's sudden demise the more touching is that only 15 months ago he followed the remains of his life's partner (Mrs. Yarwoed) to the grave ; and but three months ago be saw the remains of ' his son Frederick laid to rest within the cemetery. He leaves one son,Mr. Walter Yarwood, with whom general sympathy is expressed. The deceased was attende Iby Dr. Ormsby. I The mortal remains of Mr. S. Yarwood were com- mitted to the grave in Runcorn Cemetery on Tuesday afternoon. The funeral cortege left the deceased's late residence at three o'clock, and the following tradesmen and others showed their rcapect by walking in advance of the hearse :—Messrs. J. H.Lee (Widnes), Thomas Lea, W. Miles, W. Hankinson, W. Nieltam (huilder), Arthur Rodgers, C. Dutton, F. Dutton, W. Nickson (Church. street), H. Dekorke, Luke Holmes, May, F. Pearson, W. Brown, R. Abraham, J. Johnson, S. Spann, B. Aston and W. Bankes (undertaker). The mourners were : Messrs. Walter Yarwood (son), Frank and Edward Yar- I wood (High Leigh), J. O'Kell, E. O'Kell; J. Roberts, and G. Booth. The service in the chapel and at the grave- side was conducted by the Rev. W. Fidoo.

DEATH OF AN OLD- RUNCORN PUBLIC

:OFFICIAL There passed away on Sunday last, az the ripe old age of 82 years, in the person of Mr. Henry Coleburn, one of Runcorn's oldest and most respected public officials. His end was peacef el and he went quietly over" to the great majority" after but a few weeks illness. Born in 1818 Mr. Coleburn came to Runcorn in 1852 ,and in May of the following year,he was appointed registrar of births and delthe for the sub-district of Runcorn, a post he retained up to as recent a date as 1895, when he re- signed owing to a serious illness. During a considerable part of his active life in Runcorn be held office as vaccination offieer and regis- trar of marriages. For several years he was secretary of the Church Institute, also libraian and col- lector of subscriptions ; and during the time he was also superintendent of the National Boys Sunday schools ; teacher of the monitor's class and as a district visitor he did good work. For nearly 20 years he was a memb3r of the Runcorn Burial Board, and was twice elected chairman of that body. He was also chairman of the Runcorn Ratepayers Association (now defunct). His connection with the sport and amusement of the town was also of an eminent and time honoured standing,and for some time he held the presidency of the Runcorn Bowling Club. He also filled the position of Market Hall Inspector for a period of 14 years.—On Wednesday the funeral took place, add was largely attended. The funeral took place at the Runcorn Cemetery, on Wednesday afternoon. Prior to proceeding to the grave- side, a short service was conducted at Trinity Church by the Rev. C. R. Killick, M.A. Mr. H. Co3kerlyne (organist of Kirkstall Church Leeds), presided at the organ. The order of the cortege from the church to the Cemetery was coach, containing, Rev Canon Wood (vicar), Rev C R Killick (vicar of Trinity) and Dr Ormsby ; the hearse, accompanied by Maser:. J France, C Faulkner,W Jones, and D Bailie (bearers) •, and the remaining coaches con- taining :—Mrs H Coleburn (widow), Mss Coleburn,Mrs Blakemore, Mrs Cockerlyne, Mrs Waterworth (daugh- ters), Mr Cockerlyne (son-in-law), Mr John Maggs and Mr Harry Cockerlyne, (grandsons), Mrs Seddon (grand- daughter),Mr Seddon,sars Beauchamp (sister-in-law),Mrs Woodhouse,Mr Sutton.Mr W Hankinson,Mr Collenee,Mr Turner, Mr. Coe. and Mr. Nixon. At the graveside he concluding portion of the service was improssively read by the Rev C R Killick. The funeral arrangements were carried out by Mr W Yarwood.

NEW SCHOOLS AT BANK QUAY. MISS GERTRUDE C-R-OSFIELD LAYS THE FOUNDATION STONE.

Wednesday was an important day to all connected with the Workingmen's Mission at Bank Quay, for it was then that the foundation stone of their new schools was hit The present schools are in dose proximity to the works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons. Limited, and the extenson of the works premises necessitate tneir removal. The schools ware originally built by Mr. John Crosfield, J.P., hut on the formation of the com- pany his interest in them as an owner came to an end. Dotermined that the good work which had been carried on for so many years at the schools shou'd not be im- peded, Mr. Crosfield generously came forward and de- cided to build new schools at his own expense. An admirable site was found in Thewlis-street, off Liverpool-road, and the buildings which are now in course of erection vr:11 b 3 used for both day and Sunday Schools, as is the case with the present schools. The day school his been used for the ,ed JeaVon of infants, and has been u idw the manage- m r.it of the Committee in charge of the People's College. The new buildings erecting in Thewlis-street will coasist of one large central room and three class zooms, with cloakroom and lavatory accommodation for all the chil- dren who will attend the school. The large room will b ) used for the purposes of the Workingmen's Miss'on and Sunday School, and can be divided across the c mtre during the week when it is in use for school purposes. The room will measnre 52ft. by 32ft., and his a higlit of Mt. 9in. to collar ceidng. One classroom measures 31f c. by lBfd., and two oth 18ft. by 15f each. Toe contract for the whole of the work has been let to ' Messrs. Beckett and Co., of Warrington and Hartford. The buildings will be heated with hot water, but will have supplementary fireplaces in the classrooms. The elevations will be of Ituabon brick with stone dressings. The buildings are being eracte I from designs and unier th, superintenglence of Messrs. William aad Sager Owen, architects of Warrington. .

EA RLESTOWN WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

Tha winter eessioo in connec`ion with the above two- ciation was opened on Monday, in the Co-operative Hall. There was s very lays(' aVendsoce of members,nresided over by Mrs. Rawlins, and the meeting was very enthuai s stic Mrs. Rawlins delivered an opening address which was ne-essarilv short owing to her recent severo illness. Mr. G. W. Rawlins also spoke on political questions and asked " What has the present Government done for the workingman P" Not much.he said. Mr. Balfour had certainly introduced a Local Government Bill for Ire- land, but the landlord., of whom many were in the house of Lo-ds would have thousands added to their incomes from their Irish estate% and it did not go into the poor man's pockots. They had talk' d of old age pen- sions, but they had not becnm an aecomplished fact. The Workmrn's Compensation Bill was good so far as it went, but it only touched half the people. He believed in compensation if a man fell off a shed, he also believed in it if he fell off a haystack, if it occurred in the course of his emnloyment. Speaking of the Governtssent'a Foreign Policy. Lord Salisbury had made graceful concessions, and had allowed France to take no.seseion of Madagascar, and now they had shut their doors to our commerce, and we used to do a very great business with them. fie had Os, allowed other powers to eo into China, and very soon there would be trouble there over its parti•isn. We had lost Power, prestige and possessioss since thie Government came into power. One good thing,however, the Govern- ment had done was to strFnethen the navy and every good Liberal could not but admit that that WAS a neces- sary precaution and would not object to it. The Women's Liberal Association was a great power, and he hoped they would go on and learn more about the affairs and politice of the country. Miss &Hatt proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Rawlins, and said she was glad to be present. She had learned a areas' deal. The meetines were useful as they kept them in touch with many things they had not time to read t about. She congratulated Mrs. Bawling upon her re- covery and hoped her health would continue better and enable her to come to the meeings threughout the Winter,Miss Greenell seconded,and the vote was carried with acclamation. Letters of condolence were orlered to be rent to Mrs. Prescot in her bereavement, and to Councillor Gilmour, upon the death of his wife. RUGBY FOOTBALT.

Warrington entered the field on .Saturday with a re- freshing confidence in themielves. Their antagonists were the Morecambe team, the present possessors of the "wooden spoon," and promising candidates for its pos- session at the end of the present season. They may not win it after all, for at present Stockp art stand the best chance, having offered themselves far slaughter with a greater meekness thee the ssabiders. Some people tell me' that Warrington may find the wooden spoon be- stowed on them-as a badge of men t wiaen this season closes, but Ido not anticipa-e such an event myself. Warrington may be pirforming poorly but they are even yet a cut above Morecambe, ai SAturday's victory by twelve points to nil clearly shows.

As I have already remarked Warrington were pretty confident of success in this encoumer. and their confid- ence was due to the fact; th it they have never had any difficulty so far in beating the blue and wilt -•s twins a season. Morecambe are a s urdy. hard working lot of players, but they possess little idoa of scientific football as exemplified by combinatiou.

One thing whicii was very apparert on Saturday, when the teams entered the field, was the contrast in appearance between them. Ts visiliors were Smartly at- tired inclean,brigbt-coloured jersays.while the oh arring. t miens were as shabby"as they make 'ern." I am out of taste with the Warrington jersey altogether. The colours themselves are excellent but they are badly displayed. The stripes do not show up very well when the jerseys are new, but after a week's play and a wash or two they are dismal in the extreme. At present tho players look as if they had to tura out in last year's ' jerseys, so faded and merged into one another are the colours. A better way of snowing up the primrose and blue would b 3 to have the jeraeys dark blue, with a couple of—or one—broad binds of primrose across the cheit and round the sleeves. This is the style of the Wakefield Trinity jeraem, their colours being blue and red,ancl the effett is extremely striking. Stripes do not contrast the colour properly. whereas a broad band of yellow acros.s the blue wou'd transform the Warrington jersey into as pretty a club coatumi al any in the Northern Union. The colours cot the chile are excellent, but shown in their present form their beauty is lost en- tirely. A 9 the spectators have been free in their critic. ism of the Warrington t lam's neglected appearancs, the committee might do a far worse thing then smarten up the appearance of the Mea when they smarten the team itself by the importation of new blood.

But to return to the match itself. The principal event was the non-appearance of Barber, who had been placed on the teen tie inteniel to turn out, but a consulta- .tion with his doctor resulted in him being ordered to remain inactive, as far as foothill is concerned, for the whole of the season. Barber's general heath is unsatis- factory, and he would be foolish indeed were he to play in defiance of the medical advice. Ilk logs is a severe one to the club, but now that it is definitely known the, be will not be available, immediate efforts should be made to find his ducce3sor. At present there is a woeful scarcity of nom/went threequarters.

Morecambe usually give Warrington a better game at Wilderspool than away, and on Saturday they were no mean antagouists up to half-rime. But for their lack of combination, the seasiders would trouble a few clubs, but as it is their tant'c3 are sc.rcely ever dangerous. Holcroft was stopped in the corner on several oacasions, hut at last succeeled in registering a try amid cheer; from the impatient spectators. It was high time too that sc•riag was started, for openings galore had been allowed te slide. Mason took the place-kick, and semis ied everyoody by kicking a splendid goal from touch. Moreaamhe had saarcely a look in now, and Bate got over at thn oppo- site corner, only to be ruled in toulh. As a football display the game was an exesediagly poor one. The h3mesters play-d a really shocking game, considering the class of opponents they had to deal with Had Oldham or S wint in been the visitors, Warrington would have shown form of a vastly different character. The opening meeting of the Winter Seision of the Field Cab, was held in the Museum lecture-room, on Friday evening last week. Thera was a good attendance, and aft3r the usual routine bnsiness, the President, Mr. J. Shaw Green, gave an address in which, after commenting on the past work and future prospects of the club,he endeavoured to justi- fy his position as an opponent of evolution, The address was fair and temperate, and was followed by a long and lively discusFion, during which Mr. Green was congratulated on the literary skill with which he had treated his subject and the courage he displayed in bringing it before such an audience.

WARRINGTON SWIMMERS Al LIVERP3OL. On Tuesday, at a swimming gala promoted by the Liverpool Swimming Club. for the benefit of Pro- fessor Mitchell, several Warrington swimmers were successful. Baker won, by the narrow margin of a touch, first honours ; whilst in the 50 yards open handicap, Mr. Gil. Anderton was defeated by half a yard in the final heat. With regard to Miss Anderton, she gives every promise of developing into an excellent swimmer, and Pro- fessor Barlow, whose pupil she is, has great expec.., tations as regards her future. Mr P Bewiek occupied the platform on Monday evening, and after an address on lyceum work, in which he is deeply interested gave some successful clairvoyance and psychometry.

MAXIMUM PENALTY INFLICTED.

At the Warrin.zton Borough Police Court, on Monday morning, before the Mayor (in the chair), and other magistrates. George Thorntoo, a cripple, and described as a hawker, of no settled abode, was charged with hav- ing been drunk and disorderly in Buttermarket-street on Saturday night, and with having assaulted John Massey, a horeekeeper employed by the Warrington Carriage Company, at the sam time and place.—John Massey stated that at half past six on Saturday evening he was in Buttermarket-street in pursuit of his duty, waiting to change horses, when prisoner came up and hit him across the leg with a stick. HQ asked him not to do it again. One of the prisoner's mates then hit him in the eye and knocked him to the floc.. Before he could get up prisoner hit him on the hehd with the stick. The stick, a heavy one with an iron ferule, was banded to the magistrates). Witness said the prisoner was an absolute seranger to him. When he (witness) was struck he became almost insensible. With the assistance of some bystanders prisener was taken to the Police Station. Witness'e wound was dressed at the Police Station, and subsequently he went to the Infirmary. Five stitches were put in the wound. He lost a great quantity of blood. Prisoner was drunk at the time.—Pi ismer said he had no recollection of having done anything of the kind.—Alderman Harrison: It is very convenisnt to have no recollection of disagree- able circumstances.—Prisoner : Why should I strike him P I don't know the man.—lsaac Potter, Dye-houses, gave evidence as to the assault by the prisoner.— Walter Bancroft 35, Albert-terrace, said he also saw prisoner deliberately strike Massey on the head with a stick. If prisoner had not been a cripple he (w:tness) would have struck him. He assisted in taking him to the Pol7ce Station. He was very rough.—The Chief Con- stable observed that prisoner was a stranger, but there WIS no doubt that he was an old offender. The Bench had power to commit him to gaol for three months.— Alderman Harrison : fie Is a dangerous man to be at large.—Mr. Birchall: He is a wild beast —Prisoner was sent to gaol for a month for bring drunk and disorderly and for two months for the aseault.—The Mayor thanked Potter and Bancroft for their assistance and they were allowed 2s. 61., each on the recommendation of the Chief Constable.

BARNUM AND BAILEY'S SHOW. Although many had seen Bernurn and Bailey's "Greatest show on earth," previous to its visit to Warrington on Saturday, yet the vast majority had not. The visit of the show, resulted in a perfect exodus of visitors from the outlying country dis- tricts and small towns. The show began to arrive early in the morning and everything was fixed for the afternoon performance. A magnificient pro- cession paraded the principal streets in the t6wn about ten o'clock. A tremendous number of people visited the show at the two performances which were on an elaborate scale. Their perfect system of working and management,which is observable in the smallest details, must be credited with the fact that at the conclusion of each perfarraancs the large number of visitors were able to leave the show without any inconvenience. In a few hours after the evening performance, the visiting show- men; their mighty erection and all the innumer- able accessories to their business had departed—like nomads of the desert—on their visit to Chester which was their next place of call.

1.0.G.T. No. 1 CONVENTION, SOUTH-WEST LANCASHIRE. The monthly meeting of the above was held in Barrack-yard Mission, Market-street, Hindley, on Saturda3. Representatives were present from different parts of the area. Brother J. Taylor,C.C.T. (Earlestown) opened the proceedings at 3-45 p.m. The various sub-committees having been appointed, the report of the secretary was read by Brother Williams (Leigh), showing the lodges to be in fairly good condition, and urging the lodges to be more fraternal in their visits to One another. The reports of the Executive Committee showed that arrangements were being made to open anew lodge at Golborne. Visits had been made by the Commit- tee to Warrington and Tyldesley. It was decided to hold the next meeting at Wigan, under the aus- pices of the "Nil Desperandum" Lodge. The Con- vention resolved that a letter of sympathy be sent to Bro. Eccles, P.G. Treasurer, on the death of his wife, Sister Eccles, P.G.V.T. Sister Firth (Leigh) very feelingly took leave of the Convention on her leaving the district for North-East Lancashire. The C.C.T. suitably replied on behalf of the Convention, and wished her God-speed and success in her new sphere of labour. Brother Francis (St. Helens) proffered his twvices with a lime-li2ht lantern to the various lodges in the area. The Secretary, Bro. Lodge (Ashton), contributed a paper on " The history and mystery of ardent spirits." The thanks of the Convention were given to him for the paper, and to the Hindley members for the arrangements made for the session. Atter tea a public entertain- ment was held in the Wesleyan Schools. Brother Vint, Ashton, presided, and songs, &c., were given by various members.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of th age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured other thousands. Even some of tha best medi- cinal preparations. however much they may do to re- move one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the preva- lent diseases of this country, which wil be certain to do good when fairly tried,which will be equally adapted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the pro- fessional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand,the miner, and the roadeide labourer.lt should be, too, such a preparation as contains so injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady. as well as by the 'stronger constitu- ted man. Such a digcovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would , indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. I Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved,it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried,has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicine has failed to give relief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently benefi cial, when other preparations at best only gave tempor- ary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache,Pains in the Sidg., Giddiness,Loss of Appetite,also for,Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleeplessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits, and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an Illness, long confinement to ill-ventilated rooms, or any other cause. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts of the system which have been weakened by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease.lf you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excel- lent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles at 2s 93. and 45.61. eack,but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the Proprietors will forward same for the above prices, carriage free. Avoid Imitations. When purchasing, see the name, " Gwilvm Evans," on Label, Stamp, and Bottle. Sole proprietors : Quinine Bitters Manufacturing Company, Limited, Llanelly, South Wales.

A MILLION 1:3101NICAS FROA A MILLION SUBSCRIBERS.

Sir,—ln his debehtful hook on Palestine Edwin [ladle tells us that tire fi at words he heard on landing at Jaff3 were, give us Ba kshessh ; that he flerCV3 words wore sounded in his ears from hour to hour.day by day, from that time forth ; and that when he left Palestine af cer wandering through the land, the last thing he heard as the boat bore hirn away from its sacred shores was the echo of the cry Backsheresh. (Oa Holy ground,page 6). Well,we are not strangers to a somewhat similar cry in this country,but it does not proof el from "the poor et the land," but from a quarter where we should least ex- pect it,even from the learned and comfortable preachers of the Gonorl. The cry of these excellent men for money, more money,rings in our ears wherever we go. Ills con- tinually sounding from the pulpit and the platferm; the religious journals never weary of publishing it, and it stares us in the face from big posters on the walls of our cities and towns. The latest and, perhaps. most gigantic development of this cry is the call now being made by the Wesleyan Methodists for a million of guineas from a million of subecribers ! Whether the idea of this tn• mendous undertaking originated in a lay or in a clerical mind I know not, but I understand the pastors are throwing themselves into it with great heartiness, and , when they put their hands to the plough there is no I turning back. But does it not seem tatrange.and perhaps a little unkind, that these good moo should turn their backs for a time to the masses of sin and urrow by which they are surrounded and devote their attention to the financial affairs of their denomination. And then, is not a call for a million guinta3 a strange demand to make upon people who are ourposed to have been taught by the preachers to lay up for themselves treasures in heaven, where mo'h nor dust doth corrupt-, and where thieves do not break through nor steal (Matt. vi. 20). But the preachers are not lacking in astuteness, and know very well that in their denomination, as well as in others, there are not a few believers who aim at making the most of both worlds,that is to have treasure. on earth as well as in heaven at the same time. flow very easy it will be for people so favourably situated to draw cheques upon their earthly deposits without, in any way, trenching upon their treasury in another world. But what abut "the poor of the firck"the men who have to support themselves and their families upon wages ranging from fifteen shilling,' a week and upwards? Does it not seem both unjust and cruel to attempt to extract from thelr small earning contrib 1- Mons equal in amount to tho-e of the rich men who I neither toil nor spin, Besides,with pew rants and other imposts of tt:ei chapel they are heavily enough taxed at present, and out of consi ler.tion for their poverty the preachers might be content with widow's mites from them, if they cannot see their way to exempt then alto- gether. But what is this enormous collect:oo wanted for ? I suppose the answer is, for sprraling the Gospel, or, in other words, for building more chapels and hiring more pastors. Well, with all due deferrnes to our Methodist Mande I think it may be truly said that She Lord himself never sped or 8-motioned. such methods for extending His Gospel. Hl9 whole life and ministry prove this be- yond dispute. He was the bast and moat earaeat preacher that the world has ever seen or ev. r will are ; and yet the seashore, the mountain side, and the village street were the pulpits he mo,t frequently used. And then Jesus was nacre than a preacher: he was also a healer " of all manner of sickness and all manner of digeaaa among the people." (Matt. iv. 23) But it must never be forgotten that all his preachinw, and all his wonderful works of mercy to the bodies of men I were performed without money and without price Nor were his apostles, the humble men who parted with all their earthly possessions to follow him, hir)lin,:a in any sense of the word. No, no. At tam command of their beloved Master, they went forth without puree or scrip to evangelise a world lying in the da-knees of heathen- ism ! And how they supported themselves while thus employed may be inferred from Paul's farewell to the elders of Ephesus. In that manly and pathetic: addresa the aged Apostle Says, "I have coveted no man's silver, or gold, or apparel. Yea, ye younelves know, that these hands have ministered unto my nrc saicies, and to them that were with me. I have showed you all things, how that so labouring ye ought to s apport the weak, and to remember the words of the Lord Jesus, how he Sail, " It is nv.re blessed to give than to reoeive " (Acts xx , 33. 34, 35). knd besides all this th,re is the fart that in the New Testameot, which embraces the first century of the C stistian Church there is only one Collection mentioned, but it was f t the relief of the poor asinta in Jerusalem at a time of famine. (acts xi., 29, Romans xv., 26). Now as all these things were pat upon record for exemplary Purposes, add not as mere facts of hia!ory, does not the cry of the preachers for a million of guineas, betray a sad departure from the mind and ways of Jesus and his apostles. Money never did, and never will, have any part in the salvation of men. But alas. it does provide appliances for a fashionable religion through which the Church and rhe chap may fraternize with the world. and thereby avoid the loss and persecu- tion which seem to be the t (wren appointed lot of all who take up their cross and follow Christ.--.Youre truly, lONA.

PRESENTATION TO THE CLERK TO GREAT SANKEY SCHOOL BOARD. A special meeting of the School Board was held on Tuesday afternoon, in the Board-room, Grest Sankey, for the purpose of passing the accounts for the year ending 29th September, 1898, There were present: Mr. Thomas Halton (in the chair), the Rev E. S. Jackson, and Nit., Joseph Wright. The Clerk (Mr. W. D. Jeans, jun.l. and Mr. G. Clarke, master, were also Present. The accounts were ex- amined, and on the motion of Mr. Wright. seconded by the Rev. E. S. Jackson, were passel. The Chair- man said he had a very pleasing duty to perform now the ordinary business of the meeting was over. He wished to take this opoortnnity to congratulate Mr. Jeans, the Clerk, on his mnrriage, and on be- half of the members of the Board and teachers to present to him a slight token of their respect. and esteem. He was sure the work of the Board had always beon presented to them in a clear busines; like manner, and the books gave evidence of great care. He wished Mr. Jeans a happy and prosperous married life, and hoped that his connection with the School Board might be a long and pleasant .one. The Chairman passed to Mr. Jeans a solid silver inkstand of a chaste design, which had been sup- plied by Messrs. Woods and Son, Stmkoy-streer.— Te Rev. E. S. Jackson supported toe remarks of the Chairman, and said he was pleased to tes,ify to the ability and care the Clerk had shown in the . work of the Board, and also wished him much hap- piness in his married life —Mr. Wright also warmly endorsed the words of the previous speakers in all their good wishes for his welfare.—Mr. Jeans feel- ingly and suitably responded, and said that the present came upon him as a surprise. He felt very much the kind expressions the Chairman and mem- bers had made towards his wife and himself, and thanked them and the teachers for the valuable present they had given him.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, OCTOBER 14 1898.

EARLESTOWN A.ND NEWTON. EARLESTOWN AND DDITRICT P.S.A. SOCIETY. This society opened its session on Sunday afternoon by a large gathering in the Co-operative Hall. There was a very large attendance for the initial meeting which was presided over by Mr. T. Williamson, who delivered what might be termed an inaugural address. Addresses were also given by Mr. Jackson and Mr. S. Dring, and the choir ren- dered several selections. Mr.S.Dring presided at the organ.

SUDDEN' DEATH AT WARGRA.TE.

On Saturday, at noon, a man named James Ireland, employed as a fitter at the Vulcan Foundry, and who resided at 189, Wargrave-road, Barlestown, died in a very sudden manner. He was 56 years of age, and had been suffering from tromboA3 for the past 10 years, and had been attended by Dr. Mouncey. Deceased left home about 5.45 in the morning to go to his work, lie worked until 12 noon, and was on his way home when he was suddenly seized with illness. Be sat co urn on the embankment near Bridge's farm, and complained to some of his shop mats who ran for stimulants, but he died about 12.20. The Coroner was notified, but he did not think it necessary to hold an inquest. SUDDEN DEATH OF Slit, GEORGE LAMBERT, EARLESTOWN.

On Saturday afternoon 51r. George Lambert died very suddenly from failure of the heart's action. The deceased had been unwell of late,and last week be suffered from diarrhma but no serious conse- quences were anticipated. Ou Saturday afternoon he got up from the couch on which he had been lying, but when in the back kitchen he swooned and "immediately expired. He was the eldest son of Mr. John Lambert, boot and shoe dealer, Oxford- street, and leaves a widow but no family. He was interred at the Cemetery on Wednesday afternoon, and there was a very large number of wreaths sent by sorrowing relatives and friends. DEATH 07' AIRS. GILMOUR.

It is with regret that we have this week to an- nounee the entirely unexpected death of Mrs. Gil- mour,wife of Councillor Gilmour. which took place on Sunday afternoon at the age of 55 years. About three weeks ago Mrs. Gilmour returned from Black- pool where she had been staying, and she was then slightly indisposed. Nothing serious however was anticipated, but on Saturday the let inst she took to her bed,and was attended by Dr. Patterson. She was thought to be improving. On Sundav,however, Dr. Patterson stated that pneumonia had set in,and just after dinner the unfortunate lady suddenly expired to the extreme surprise and grief of all her relatives. She was the third daughter of the late Mr. Josiah Harrison, and in her early days was schoolmistress of the old Vitriol school.

THE PIT SHAFT FATALITY AT ST,

HELENS.

COLLIERY OFFICIALS CENSURED. On Tuesday Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held an inquest at the Railway Hotel, Sutton, St. Helens, on the body of William Cowley, 26 'years of age, collier, of 108, B ary's-lane, who was killed in the shaft of the Ashton's Green Colliery, St. Helens, on Saturdty morn- ing. Mr. H. Hall, Government inspector of mines, and Mr. Matthews,assistant Government inspector,were pre- sent; Mr J. 0. Swift, solicitor, and Mr. T. Pennington certificated manager, appeared for Messrs. Bromilow, Foqter and Co., the colliery proprietors ; Mr. H. L. Riley, solicitor, representad the relatives of the deceased and Mr. T. Glover, J.P., miner's agent also attended. The Coroner informed the jury that Cowley and an- other collier named Tarpey were baing wound up the shaf t when, through some cause, a coalbox fell down thia shaft. It dropped upon the cage in which the men were, with the result that the top was smythed in, Cowley being knocked out of the cage and dropping to the battorn of the shaft, where he WAS afterwards found dead, and Tarpey was rendered unconscious, thought he rein lined in the cage. Fie was found in that condition when the cage reached the surface, and he was removed to the hospital.

After evidence of inlentification hal been given by Julia Cowley, widow, Joseph Buckley, of Fleet-lene, said he was the browman at Ashton's Green Colliery, and was in chirge of the shaft arrangements at the time of the accident, whith was about three o'clock on Situ!- clay morning.—The Coroner: Flow do you aeceunt for a tub filling down the shaft ?—Witness: My own opinion is that it must have fillers from the came. I must have put one in and forgotten it.—The Coroner: When you put the box in the cage did you lower the arm, or protecting bar, to keep the box in the cage ? Witnees : I don't remember that I did. I never make a practice of sending one empty down the shalt.—The Coroner : Is the enginernan entitled to start winding without getting any signal from you ? Witness : He should not be, but if I am away from the pit mouth he does so.—ln reply to Mr. Matthews, witness said it was the custom for the enginemen to accept and act upon signals from the bottom of the shaft during the night time when there was not much winding, but it was never done in the day time. He knew there was a rule that bankamee should attend to and remain near the shaft when men were descending or ascending.—ln answer to Mr. Glover, witness sail ther-3 were no re- eeiviag"catches"at thn surfact.Tne ems worke without the catches all the time.—A Juror: The accident wou'd not have happened if there hal been "catchel"at the top.because the cage couldn't have descended unless the brewman was there to release the "catches?' Joseph Lyon, eneineman and winder at the time of the accident,said it had always been the custom to start winding Oct receiving the signals from the bottom of the shaft, and in the absence of the banksman in the night ttimec.o—nTheuCocro4nseifr t lesaidy that might lead to very danger- ous en were allowed to do that. They could not tell whether the banksman was ready or not, or what he was ding at the top of the shaft.—The Witnese said it was always customary to leave the cage ready for winding before the banksman went away.— Coroner asked if the certificated manager knew of this. He told witness to never mind the manager being ore.. gent at that inquiry—they would not crscharge him, and if they did he(the coroner) would b 3 there. Did the certificated manager know--Mr.Swift : I don't think that is either a very judicious or judicial remark to make,Mr.Ceroner.—The Coroner :Mill you kindly allow me to ask the quash i,n 2-11 r. Swift : I think I have a right to protest against a remark of that kind.—The Coroner : I saw the witness looking towards the certificeter manager when he was about to answer.— Mr. Swift.—Yee held a very high offin and sit here in a judicial capacity, and I protect againct you telling the witness that the manag,er---The Coroner (interrupt- Will you allow me to examine the witness?-11.1r. Swift : If I think you are wrong I shall rir ;test against it.—The Coroner (to Vi imese) : You tell me—Did tile certified manager know nr not know that this system of winding was going on F—Witness : Yes.

Mr. M-atthew-a, 'assistant inspector, said he examined the place of the accident, and thought the accident might have been caused by the baaksnaan failing to put on the "catches" of the &needing caee. there was uaually same latitude allowed where banks men had little to do during the night, but it was advis- able that under no circumstances should men be lowered OT raised without the hanksman being present. The jury, after deltberating in private, returned a verdict of " Accidental death," with the presentment that the management, knowing the system of signalling and windinz in the night time had been carried on for two years or mere,should see that the rules were carried Properly and according to law. They further presented that there was great carelessness on the part of the banksman and engine winder,—The coroner said he en- tirely agreed with the verdict and presentment of the jary, and censured the two men, Buckley and Lvon,for their share in the matter.—Mr. Swift, on behalf of the colliery proprietors. Said that the presentment of the jury, so far as it affected them, would see that it was observed and carried nut.

A new 'bus-wheel tyre is being tried In London. It con • stets of an ordinary !Woe with a channelled rim. Into this a rubber ring is sunk, and outside the rubber ring is a kind of chain tyre composed of a series of 3in steel links. The wear of the road is taken by the steel, and vibration is stated to be absorbed by the rubber cushioning tinamediately be - neath.

Barnstaple on Monday morning, George Harper, a blacksmith, formerly a well-known rifle chot, committed suicide. The large dwelling house, all the farm build- ings. and fourteea stacks of corn were burnt to the ground. Twelve valuable horses, and some pigs, and about sixty fowls perished. The tenant (Mr. Sharp) and his family were assay from boots at the time of the outbreak. The fire is supposed to be the work of an incendiary.

AA English strike-anywhere harmless match appears to have bean invented and patented some months ago by Mr. Cordes, an employbe of the London County Council. The ingredients of the pasta used comprise potassium chlorate, whiting. plaster-of-Paris, ground glass, glue, and amorphous phosphorus. The relative quantities of each material are as yet not stated : incorrectly apportioned, they produce an easily-exploded mixture. I Ancient mirrors made in Roman times are found occasion- ally when exoavations ars proceeding below the sites of 'orrner cities. A systematic examination of a number Of these glasses hp M. Berthelot. has disclosed that they were often backed with untarnished lead, and not with quicksilver or tin. The reflections given are, in some instances. still r quite passable, although time has been trying for 1,003 pears or so to introduce oxygen and dull the bri,:ht surface of the metal in contact with the glass.

•- A tragic affair is reported from Prod& in Prance. A well- to-do young farmer named Francois Pellegrin was engaged to be married to Felicc Barbarnu. a pretty girl of 18. A few lays ago he received an anonymous le' ter making a foul im- putation against his sweetheart, who, in spite of his protests that he did not believe a word, was so overcome that she hanged heraelf during the night in a barn. Her lover was was distracted at her suicide. and only a few hours after her body had been cut down he went out to the barn and blew out his brains. The police are looking for the writer of the anonymous letter. Serious strike disturbances took pla‘e on Wednesday at Virden, Illinois. The strikers on the one side, and the Sheriff and his deputies and a force of railway police on the other, fought a desperate battle, during which no fewer than 630 shots were exchanged. Ten of the combatants are known to have been killed, and five fatally and five others seriously wounded. Later renorts, believed, however, to bs exaggerated. put the loss of life at a high figure—one account stating that fifty miners and six of the police were killed and another, which says the fighting was being continued that over 100 In all have been killed. • _

The land crabs of Cuba are 1,;;;;: than a sea crab, and live entirely on the land. They run with great speed, even out- stripping a horse. At certain seasons of the year they mi. grate in large bodies from one side of the island to the other. in columns sem times half a mile wide, and so dense as aimed to stop a carriage on the road they may be crossing. These columns overcome every obstacle in their direct line of aiareh, even high mountains. It is supposed that these mi- zrations are promoted by the Instinct of propagation, as the vith• seek the sea shore, deposit their evgs. and cast off the )1d she 1. Th.se crabs are so oornmen about the city of Vlatansas that the inhabitants often receive the sobriquet of cangrejo." They are ftequently found in the houses, and In some cases even under the beds. There is a species of ionch which makee similar marches through the country in immense bodies. These are called pirates, from a very curi- ous habit they display. This creature, which resembles a mall, has the ablity of detaching itself fro.a the shell which for some reason, it temporarily leaves at times, and while its 'louse is thus vacant another, passing, will pack its body, tail foremost, Into the empty shell and keep possession.

THE FRENCH IN WEST AFRICA. CAPTURE OF SAMORY• AND ALL HIS GRIEFS. PARIS, THURSDAY. A despatch from St. Louis, Senegal, states that rumour is current there that Samory has been captured by the French forces. Leiut. Jacquin personally al after Samory, who was escaping, and took him prisoner. Four hundred nibs, ninety cases of cartridges, and oue cannon fell into the hands of the French. For over twenty years Samory has been fighting the French. The Capture of Lieut. Henderson, a British officer, hi Samory's son eighteen months ago will be remembered. Samory had then a large following, but the French cora' pelled him to retreat towards Liberia. He was a stricl Mohammedan, and the most powerful of the indepew dent monarchs of the Western Soudan.
